\alpha,(\tilde{F},\tilde{g}),A^{1},u,v,T_{A},P
, and
z
. Since,
(u-z,v+z)=(u^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{1})
, this yields an
\alpha
-spanning tree for
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
and
(F_{A^{1}},f_{A^{1}})
.

Conversely, suppose
t(u)\neq\ell
.
Let
z=u_{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
.
For
t\neq\ell
, we have
z_{t}=0
, and for
t=\ell
, we have
z_{t}=z^{\prime}_{t}
. Hence
z\leq z^{\prime}
.
We apply Lemma
26
to
\alpha,(\tilde{F},\tilde{g}),A^{2},u,v,T_{A},P
, and
z
. Since,
(u-z,v+z)=(u^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime}_{2})
, this yields an
\alpha
-spanning tree for
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
and
(F_{A^{2}},f_{A^{2}})
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
27
,
28
and
29
immediately yields the following corollary:

Corollary 30
.

Let
\mathscr{O}=(O,b)
be an ordered pattern over
m
vectors, index
p\in I_{\mathscr{O}}
and coordinate
\ell\in Z_{\mathscr{O}}(\ell)
and
let
(O^{1},b^{1})=\pi_{1}(O,b,p,\ell)
,
(O^{2},b^{2})=\pi_{2}(O,b,p,\ell)
. Then it holds that
\{(O,b)\}\simeq\{(O^{1},b^{1}),(O^{2},b^{2})\}
.

Subroutines.

Based on the functions
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, we formulate a subroutine that transforms a single pattern into an equivalent family of nice patterns.

We now define the first subroutine,
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
, which takes as input an ordered pattern
(O,b)
, and a bad index
i\in I_{O,b}
, and outputs a set of ordered patterns, that result from an exhaustive application of the operations
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
to all bad coordinates of
i
.

Intuitively, we obtain the
j
th ordered pattern in the output from
(O,b)
as follows. Let
z_{j}
be the
j
th smallest bad coordinate of index
i
. We shift from index
i
everything except one unit, i.e., the vector at index
i
becomes
\mathbf{e}_{z_{j}}
. The units from the first
j-1
bad coordinates are shifted to the corresponding big indices, and all other units (i.e., units from coordinates that were not bad and all except one unit from
z_{j}
) are shifted to
b(z_{j})
. If
i
was a big vector for some coordinates,
b(z_{j})
becomes the big vector for those coordinates instead. Finally, the last ordered pattern in the output is obtained by shifting all bad coordinates from
i
to the respective big vectors.

Procedure 1
.

The operation
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
takes as input a pair
(\mathscr{O},i)
, for an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}=(O,b)
with
O=O_{1},\dots,O_{m}
, and
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}}
.
Let
Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i)=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell}\}
.
The operation outputs a set of pairs
\{(\mathscr{O}^{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{\ell+1},p_{\ell+1})\}
, where
p_{j}=b(z_{j})
for
j\in[\ell]
,
p_{\ell+1}=\perp
, and
\mathscr{O}^{j}=(O^{j},b^{j})
for
j\in[\ell+1]
are ordered vectors defined as follows:
Let
v=O_{i}
.

For
j\in[\ell]
and
d\in[m]
we define the vector
w=(O^{j})_{d}
from
w^{\prime}=O_{d}
as follows. We distinguish four different cases:

•

If
d=b(z_{j})
, we define

w_{z}=\begin{cases}w^{\prime}_{z}+v_{z}&\colon z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1}\}\land b(z_{j})=b(z),\\
w^{\prime}_{z}&\colon z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1}\}\land b(z_{j})\neq b(z),\\
w^{\prime}_{z}+v_{z}-1&\colon z=z_{j},\\
w^{\prime}_{z}+v_{z}&\colon\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

•

If
d=b(z)
and
d\neq b(z_{j})
for some
z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1}\}
, we define
w_{z}=w^{\prime}_{z}+v_{z}
and
w_{z^{\prime}}=w^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}}
for
z^{\prime}\neq z
.

•

If
d=i
: we define
w=\mathbf{e}_{z_{j}}
.

•

If
d=b(z)
and
d\neq b(z_{j})
for some
z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1}\}
, we define
w_{z}=w^{\prime}_{z}+v_{z}
and
w_{z^{\prime}}=w^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}}
for
z^{\prime}\neq z
.

•

Otherwise, we define
w=w^{\prime}
.

We define
b^{j}
as the mapping such that
b^{j}(z)=b(z_{j})
for each coordinate
z
with
b(z)=i
, and
b^{j}(z)=b(z)
for all other coordinates
z
.

Finally, we define
b^{\ell+1}=b
. For
d\in[m]
, we define the vector
w=(O^{\ell+1})_{d}
from
w^{\prime}=O_{d}
as follows:

w_{z}=\begin{cases}0&\colon d=i\land z\in Z_{O,b}(i),\\
w^{\prime}_{z}+v_{z}&\colon d=b(z)\land z\in Z_{O,b}(i),\\
w^{\prime}_{z}&\colon\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

\blacktriangle

By definition,
Procedure

1
outputs ordered patterns where the index
i
is no longer bad, and the only (possibly) newly created bad index is given by the output pair. The following observation follows:

Observation 31
.

Let
(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},p)
be some pair in the output of
\operatorname{ShiftVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. Then the following points hold:

•

either
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
, or
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=(I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\})\cup p
, and

•

b^{j}([k])\subseteq b([k])
.

In particular, it holds for the unique pair
(\mathscr{O},p)
with
p=\perp
that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
.

Lemma 32
.

Given an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}=(O,b)
and an index
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}}
. Let
P
be the set resulting from
\operatorname{ShiftVectorL}(\mathscr{O},i)
and let
S=\{\mathscr{O}\colon(\mathscr{O},p)\in P\}
. Then
S
is equivalent to
\mathscr{O}
.

Proof.

Let
Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i)=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{t}\}
, and let
\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{t+1}
be the set of ordered patterns as defined in
Procedure

1
.
We define the sequence of ordered patterns
(Y^{0},y^{0}),\dots,(Y^{\ell},y^{\ell})
, where
(Y^{0},y^{0})=(O,b)
, and
(Y^{j},y^{j})=\pi_{2}(Y^{j-1},y^{j-1},i,z_{j})
for all
j\in[\ell]
. Then it holds for each
j\in[\ell]
that
\mathscr{O}^{j}=\pi_{1}(Y^{j-1},y^{j-1},i,z_{j})
. We show by induction over
j
that
\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{j}\}\cup\{(Y^{j},y^{j})\}
is equivalent to
\{(O,b)\}
. The lemma then follows from the observation that
\mathscr{O}^{\ell+1}=(Y^{\ell},y^{\ell})
.

For
j=0
the claim holds trivially since
(Y^{0},y^{0})=(O,b)
.
Assume now that the claim holds for
j-1
, i.e., that
\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{j-1}\}\cup\{(Y^{j-1},y^{j-1})\}
is equivalent to
\{(O,b)\}
. Since it holds that
\mathscr{O}^{j}=\pi_{1}(Y^{j-1},y^{j-1},i,z_{j})
and
(Y^{j},y^{j})=\pi_{2}(Y^{j-1},y^{j-1},i,z_{j})
, it holds again by
Corollary

30
that
\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{j}\}\cup\{(Y^{j},y^{j})\}
is equivalent to
\{(O,b)\}
.
∎

Based on this operation, we define the recursive procedure
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
that we use as a subroutine in our final process that turns any given pattern into an equivalent family of nice patterns. Informally, the
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
operation selects an index
i
and outputs a family of patterns where
i
is no longer a bad index, and no other indices become bad.

Procedure 2
.

The recursive operation
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
takes as input an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}
over
m
vectors and an index
i\in[m]
, and outputs a family of ordered patterns as follows:

If
O_{i}
is not a bad vector with respect to
b
, then we output the family
\{\mathscr{O}\}
.
Otherwise, let
\{(\mathscr{O}^{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{\ell+1},p_{\ell+1})\}=\operatorname{ShiftVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
, and let
(\mathscr{O}^{\ell+1},p_{\ell+1})
be the unique pair with
p_{\ell+1}=\perp
.
Then for
j\in[\ell]
, we define
C_{j}=\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{j},p_{j})
.
We define the output of the operation as
C=\{\mathscr{O}^{\ell+1}\}\cup\bigcup_{j\in[\ell]}C_{j}
.

Now we prove some properties of this operation that we need later to prove the correctness of our procedures. We start with the following definition:

Definition 33
.

Let
(O,b)
be an ordered pattern over
m
vectors and
i\in[m]
. We define the tree
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
as the recursion tree of the operation
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O,b,i)
(we omit the ordered pattern
O,b
when clear from context). We also label each node of
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
corresponding to a call
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})
with the index
i^{\prime}
.

Observation 34
.

For a recursion tree
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
and a node
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})
corresponding to a child of the root of
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
, all the following points hold:

1.

(O^{\prime})_{i}=\mathbf{e}_{d}
for some
d\in Z_{O,b}(i)
, and hence,
i\notin I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
,

2.

(b^{\prime})^{-1}(i)=\emptyset
,

3.

i^{\prime}=b(d)
for some
d\in Z_{O,b}(i)
, and

4.

b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b([k])
.

Lemma 35
.

There cannot exist two distinct nodes on the same root-leaf path of the recursion tree
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
sharing the same label
j\in[m]
.

Proof.

By induction over the depth of the nodes in the recursion tree, and by items (2) and (4) of
Observation

34
, for a node corresponding to a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})
, and for a descendant node corresponding to a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime},i^{\prime\prime})
, it holds that
(b^{\prime\prime})^{-1}(i^{\prime})=\emptyset
. The lemma follows then by item (3) of
Observation

34
.
∎

Lemma 36
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
be the recursion tree of a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O,b,i)
.
Then
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
has at most
k^{k}
nodes.
In particular, the procedure runs in time
k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
,
and it holds that
|\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O,b,i)|\leq k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Proof.

A call of
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O,b,i)
makes at most
|Z_{O,b}(i)|\leq k
recursive calls, and hence, each node of
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
has at most
k
children. Moreover, it holds by
Lemma

35
that the labels assigned to the nodes of each root-leaf path of the recursion tree
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
are pairwise distinct. Hence,
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
has height at most
k
. It follows that
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
has at most
k^{k}
nodes. The bound on the running time follows then, since each node of the recursion tree, aside from the recursive calls, only makes a call of
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
, which runs in polynomial time.

Now let
T_{h}
be the maximum size of a family returned by a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O,b,i)
with a recursion tree of height
h
. Then it holds that
T_{0}=1
, and for
h\geq 1
, it holds that
T_{h}\leq k\cdot T_{h-1}+1\leq(k+1)T_{h-1}
. This implies that
T_{k}\leq(k+1)^{k}\leq k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
∎

Lemma 37
.

Let
(O,b)
be an ordered pattern over
m
vectors and let
i\in[m]
. Then the operation
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O,b,i)
produces a family of ordered patterns equivalent to
\{(O,b)\}
, such that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{O,b}\setminus\{i\}
and
b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b([k])
for each pattern
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
in the resulting family.

Proof.

Let
(O^{0},b^{0},i_{0}),\dots,(O^{r},b^{r},i_{r})
be a sequence of calls of the
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
operation corresponding to some root-leaf path of the recursion tree
\mathcal{T}_{O,b,i}
. In particular,
i_{0},\dots,i_{r}
are the labels assigned to the nodes of this path. Intuitively, in each iteration the coordinates of
i_{j}
are shifted to either their big indices or to
i_{j+1}
, so
i_{j+1}
is the only index that can turn bad in
O^{j+1}
with respect to
b^{j+1}
. Formally, we have that
I_{O^{j+1},b^{j+1}}\subseteq(I_{O^{j},b^{j}}\setminus\{i_{j}\})\cup\{i_{j+1}\}
.
It follows by induction, that
I_{O^{r},b^{r}}\subseteq I_{O,b}\setminus\{i\}
holds for the last pattern
(O^{r},b^{r})
in the sequence.

Moreover, by
Observation

34
,
b^{j+1}([k])\subseteq b^{j}([k])
, and hence,
b^{r}([k])\subseteq b([k])
holds also by induction.
The equivalence of the families also holds by induction using
Lemma

32
, since we only replace a pattern with the family resulting from an application of
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
on this pattern.
∎

Finally, we define the
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
operation that, given a pattern
A
, outputs a family of nice patterns equivalent to
A
. The first step of this operation is a subroutine called
\operatorname{ProcessBig}
that takes care of the big vectors, i.e., ensures that the big vectors have no bad coordinates.

Procedure 3
.

We define the subroutine
\operatorname{ProcessBig}
that takes an ordered pattern
(O,b)
as input, and outputs a family of patterns as follows:
Let
H_{0}=\{(O,b)\}
. We compute
H_{j}
from
H_{j-1}
for
j\in[k]
as follows:
For each pattern
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in H_{j-1}
, we define the set
C_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
, where
C_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=\{(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\}
if
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k])=\emptyset
, and
C_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O^{\prime},b^{\prime},i)
for the smallest index
i\in I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k])
otherwise. Then we define
H_{j}=\bigcup_{(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in H_{j-1}}C_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
.

We define
H_{j}=\bigcup_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in H_{j-1}}C_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}
. Let
s
be the smallest integer such that
H_{j}=H_{j+1}
. The operation outputs the family
H_{s}
.

Lemma 38
.

It holds for each ordered pattern
(O,b)
, that
\operatorname{ProcessBig}(O,b)
runs in time
k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
and outputs a family
H
of ordered patterns equivalent to
(O,b)
, such that for each pattern
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in H
, it holds that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k])=\emptyset
,
b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b([k])
and
O_{j}=O^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\notin b([k])
. Moreover, the process finishes after at most
k
iterations.

Proof.

The last claim follows from the fact, that for each ordered pattern
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in H_{j}
with
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime},i)
for some ordered pattern
(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\in H_{j-1}
, it holds that
i\in b^{\prime\prime}([k])\cap I_{O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}}
. It holds by
Lemma

37
that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}}\setminus\{i\}
. Hence, it holds that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k])
is strictly smaller than
I_{O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}}
. Therefore, it holds by item (3) of
Observation

34
that largest size of such a set over all patterns in
H_{j}
decreases after each iteration. Hence, it must hold after
k
iterations for all ordered patterns
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in H_{j}
that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k])=\emptyset
.

The claim
b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b([k])
holds by
Observation

34
. The claim
O_{j}=O_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\notin b([k])
also holds by
Observation

34
,
since we only call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on indices in
b([k])
, which only shifts indices to
b([k])
. It holds that
H_{j}
is equivalent to
H_{j-1}
by
Lemma

37
, since we only replace a pattern with the family resulting from an application of
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on this pattern. It follows by induction over
j
that
H
is equivalent to
(O,b)
.

We claim that
|H_{j}|\leq k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
for all
j\geq 0
. The lemma then follows by
Lemma

36
. Let
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in H_{j}
, then it holds for each index
i\in I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
that

O^{\prime}_{i}=O_{i}
. Namely, each time we shift to an index
i
(possibly making it a bad index), we directly call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on
i
, and hence, by
Lemma

37
,
i
cannot be a bad index in the patterns resulting form such a call. Hence, each pattern of
H_{j}
can be uniquely identified by the indices of
b([k])
that were “reduced” (i.e., indices
i
such that
i\not\in I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
), and by their respective values. Let
S
be the set of these indices that do not correspond to unit or zero vectors. Since all the indices of
S
are not bad indices, the non-zero coordinates of their corresponding vectors must form a partition of a subset of
[k]
. Hence, we have at most
3^{k}\cdot k^{k}\cdot(k+1)^{k}=k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
possible patterns in
H_{j}
, where the first term counts for each index
i\in b([k])
whether
O^{\prime}_{i}=O_{i}
,
O^{\prime}_{i}
is a unit or zero vector, or
i\notin I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
and
O_{i}
is not a unit or zero vector. The second term counts the number of partitions of the non-zero coordinates, and the third term counts the different unit and zero vectors for each index corresponding to a unit or zero vector
O^{\prime}_{i}
.
∎

Now we are ready to describe the procedure
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
, that takes as input a pattern and outputs a family of nice patterns equivalent to the input:

Procedure 4
.

The procedure
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
takes as input a pattern
A\in\mathcal{P}
, and outputs a family of patterns.
The procedure first fixes an arbitrary ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}_{A}\coloneqq(O,b)
of
A
. It computes the family
S_{0}=\operatorname{ProcessBig}(O,b)
.

Let
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\}\coloneqq I_{O,b}\setminus b([k])
.
For
j\in[s]
, we define
S_{j}
as follows:
Let
S^{\prime}_{j}=\bigcup_{(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in S_{j-1}}\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O^{\prime},b^{\prime},i_{j})
.
Then the family
S_{j}
results from
S^{\prime}_{j}
as follows: We iterate over all patterns
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in S^{\prime}_{j}
, and we add
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
to
S_{j}
if the following two conditions hold: first if
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
contains at most one zero vector, and second if
S_{j}
does not already contain another pattern
(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
that shares the same underlying pattern with
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
, i.e.,
p(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})=p(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
. In other words, for each pattern
p\in\mathcal{P}
, we only add one “representative” ordered pattern of
p
to
S_{j}
if such a pattern exists in
S^{\prime}_{j}
.
The operation outputs the family

\big\{p(\mathscr{O})\colon\mathscr{O}\in S_{s}\big\}
.

\blacktriangle

In the following we show that this subroutine runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, and correctly produces a family of nice patterns equivalent to the input pattern.

Lemma 39
.

It holds for all
j\in[s]_{0}
and
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in S^{\prime}_{j}
that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
and
b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b([k])
. Further, it also holds that
O^{\prime}_{\ell}=O_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction over
j\in[s]_{0}
.
For
j=0
, the claim holds by
Lemma

38
, since
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=I_{O,b}\setminus b([k])
for all
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in S_{0}
. Moreover,
b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b([k])
holds by Lemma
37
.

Now assume the claim holds for
j-1
. Let
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
be a pattern in
S^{\prime}_{j}
, and let
(O^{*},b^{*})
be a pattern in
S_{j-1}
such that
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\operatorname{ReduceVector}(O^{*},b^{*},i_{j})
.
Then it holds by
Lemma

37
that

I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{O^{*},b^{*}}\setminus\{i_{j}\}
and
b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b^{*}([k])
. Moreover, it holds by the induction hypothesis that
I_{O^{*},b^{*}}=\{i_{j},\dots,i_{s}\}
, and
b^{*}([k])\subseteq b([k])
. It follows that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\subseteq\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
, and
b^{\prime}([k])\subseteq b([k])
.

Finally, the inclusion
\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}\subseteq I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
follows from the fact that
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
only shifts to big indices, and that it holds by induction hypothesis that
b^{*}([k])\subseteq b([k])
is distinct from all indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{s}
. It also holds by the same observation, that
O^{\prime}_{i_{\ell}}=O^{*}_{i_{\ell}}=O_{i_{\ell}}
for all
\ell\in\{j+1,\dots,s\}
.
∎

Corollary 40
.

For each
A\in\mathcal{P}
, the operation
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
produces a family of nice patterns equivalent to
A
.

Proof.

It holds by
Lemma

39
that
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for all
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in S_{s}
. Since we remove all patterns containing at least two zero vectors, it holds by
Observation

24
that the output family is nice.

It holds by
Lemma

38
that
S_{0}
is equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}_{A}\}
.
We show that
S_{j}
is equivalent to
S_{j-1}
for all
j\in[s]
, and hence, it holds by induction over
j\in[s]_{0}
that
S_{j}
is equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}_{A}\}
.
The family
S^{\prime}_{i}
is equivalent to
S_{i-1}
for all
i\in[s]
by
Lemma

37
. The family
S_{i}
is then equivalent to
S^{\prime}_{i}
by the fact that we only remove patterns that are either equivalent to other patterns in the family, or contain at least two zero vectors. The latter patterns are not compatible with any partial solutions, and hence, are equivalent to the empty family, and they can be removed safely.
∎

Lemma 41
.

It holds for each
j\in[s]_{0}
that
|S_{j}|\leq|\mathcal{P^{*}}|\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Proof.

For
j=0
this holds by
Lemma

38
. Now assume
j>0
.
By
Lemma

39
, all patterns in
S_{j}
have the same set of bad indices and identical vectors at bad indices, i.e., it holds for all
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in S_{j}
,
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
, and for
i\in I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
,
O^{\prime}_{i}=O_{i}
.
Let
S_{j}^{*}
be the family resulting from
S_{j}
by removing all vectors
\{O_{i}\colon i\in\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}\}
. Then
S_{j}^{*}
contains nice patterns only, each containing at most
n
vectors. Moreover, for any two patterns
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime}),(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\in S_{j}^{*}
, it holds that
p(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\neq p(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, since otherwise, the original ordered patterns in
S_{j}
would share the same underlying pattern, a contradiction to our choice of of
S_{j}
. Hence, the size of
S_{j}^{*}
(and hence, of
S_{j}
) is bounded by the number of nice patterns with at most
n
vectors (i.e.,
|\mathcal{P^{*}}|
), which is at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, by
Observation

21
.
∎

Corollary 42
.

The operation
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
for each pattern
A\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

It holds by
Lemma

38
that
\operatorname{ProcessBig}(O,b)
runs in time
k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
For each
j\in[s]
, we apply
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on each element of the family
S_{j-1}
. This runs in time
|S_{j-1}|\cdot k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}{\mathsf{poly}}(n)\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
by Lemmas
36
and
41
, since each call of the
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
runs in polynomial time.
Moreover, it holds by these two lemmas that
S^{\prime}_{j}
has size
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. Hence,
S_{j}
can be computed from
S^{\prime}_{j}
in time
(n^{\mathcal{O}(k)})^{2}{\mathsf{poly}}(n)=n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
Since
s\leq n
, the total running time is
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
∎

4.3
The Algorithm

Before defining the algorithm, we describe an auxiliary operator that corresponds to relabeling vertices in the NLC-expression, i.e., it describes how a pattern corresponding to a partial solution changes after a relabel operation.

Definition 43
.

For a relabeling function
\beta:[k]\to[k]
and for
v\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we define
\rho_{\beta}(v)=v^{\prime}
, where
v^{\prime}_{i}=\sum\limits_{j\in\beta^{-1}(i)}v_{j}
.
Given a pattern
A
, we define

\rho_{\beta}(A)=\langle\rho_{\beta}(v)\colon v\in A\rangle
.

Moreover, we define the notion of “unit components” and “big components” that will be used in the algorithm:

Definition 44
.

Given a nice pattern
A
, we call a connected component of the forest
F_{A}
a
unit component
, if it corresponds to a unit vector of
A
.
Similarly, a component of
F_{A}
is a
zero component
, if it corresponds to a zero vector. We call a connected component of
F_{A}
a
big component
, if it is neither a unit nor a zero component.

Note that while this definition closely relates to the definitions of unit and big indices in an ordered pattern, the main difference is that here we forbid big components to correspond to unit vectors, where allowing such was necessary in the definitions of big indices.

Now, we describe our algorithm. At a high level, at each join node of the NLC-decomposition, we iterate over all pairs of patterns representing partial solutions at the two children of the current node. For each such pair of patterns, we iterate over all possible ways of combining them by adding edges between the partial solutions corresponding to these two patterns. Finally, we execute the relabeling procedure on each resulting pattern, and represent the outcome with its nice equivalent using the
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
operation.

The main difficulty lies in guessing the edges between a pair of partial solutions at the children nodes. By
Observation

15
, without loss of generality we can shift the labels of one partial solution by
k
, and subsequently unify the two partial solutions. Thus we obtain a graph with labels in
[2k]
, which has at most
2k
big components, at most
n
unit components and at most two zero components.

We will add edges between these components in three stages: first, we add the edges whose both endpoints lie in big components. We follow by adding edges whose both endpoints lie in unit components. Finally, we add edges that connect a unit component to a big component. The first two types of edges can be added in a straightforward way. Namely, we can add at most
2k-1
edges between big components (adding more edges would yield a cycle), and at most one edge between two unit components (adding an edge between two unit components disconnects them from the rest of the graph, so the only case in which this leads to a solution is when the current forest consists of only two unit components). Therefore, we can simply iterate over all such choices.

However, the third type of edges, those between unit and big components, has to be treated with more care. The naive approach of iterating over all such possibilities leads to a
k^{n}
algorithm at best. Instead, we start by observing that after adding these edges, some unit components will remain unchanged, while the rest be “absorbed” by big components, without adding additional requirement to the big components they attach to. This holds, since by definition, they only have unit requirements, which are satisfied by the added edge.
In other words, the total requirement of a big component decreases by the number of unit components attached to each label, regardless of the types of the unit components attached. Therefore, we can guess two things independently:
Firstly, for each of the
2k
types of unit components, we guess how many components of each type will be absorbed. Secondly, for each label, by definition, there exists at most one vertex of this label in some big component having non-zero requirement. We then guess the number of unit vectors that will attach to each such vertex. To verify the validity of our guesses, we need to find a set of edges between unit and big components the realizes them. We achieve this by a call of a
Bipartite
b
-Matching
oracle. Although different valid solutions to the
Bipartite
b
-Matching
problem lead to different resulting forests, it holds by the argument above, that all these forests admit the same pattern. Therefore, a single solution of the
Bipartite
b
-Matching
instances suffices to construct the resulting pattern.

Algorithm 1
.

For each
x\in\mathcal{V}
, we define the families
T_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{*}
recursively over
\mathcal{T}
as follows. For an initial node
\bullet_{i}
, let
v
be the only vertex of
\bullet_{i}
; we define
T_{x}=\{\langle t\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle:\>t\in D(v)\}
.
For a join node
x\in\mathcal{V}
with
G_{x}=G_{x_{1}}\oplus_{\alpha}^{\beta}G_{x_{2}}
, we define
P_{x}=\{(A_{1},A_{2})\colon A_{1}\in T_{x_{1}},A_{2}\in T_{x_{2}}\}
.
The algorithm computes the table
T_{x}
in three steps, where it first computes two intermediate tables
T^{(1)}_{x}
and
T^{(2)}_{x}
as follows:

First, we define the table
T^{(1)}_{x}
.
For each pair
(A_{1},A_{2})\in P_{x}
, let
F_{1}:=F_{A_{1}}
, and
F_{2}
be the forest obtained from
F_{A_{2}}
by relabeling each vertex of label
i
to label
k+i
for all
i\in[k]
, and let
\alpha^{\prime}=\{(i,k+j)\colon(i,j)\in\alpha\}
. Let
\hat{G}
be the disjoint union of
F_{1}

and
F_{2}
, and let

\hat{E}=E(F_{1}\oplus_{\alpha^{\prime}}F_{2})\setminus E(\hat{G})
,
i.e.
\hat{E}
is the set of edges added to
\hat{G}
by
\alpha^{\prime}
. Finally, let
f:[2k]\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
be defined as
f(i)=f_{A_{1}}(i)
for
i\in[k]
and
f(i)=f_{A_{2}}(i-k)
for
i\in\{k+1,\dots,2k\}
.

Let
B
be the set of vertices in big components with degree requirement at least one, and
U
be the set consisting of the unique vertex of requirement one in each unit component. For each subset
E^{\prime}\subseteq\hat{E}
containing at most
2k-1
edges between vertices of
B
, and at most one edge between vertices of
U
and no other edges, let
G^{\prime}
be the graph resulting from
\hat{G}
by adding the edges of
E^{\prime}
. If it holds that
G^{\prime}
is acyclic, and it holds for all vertices
v\in\hat{G}
that
\mathrm{deg}_{E^{\prime}}(v)\leq f(v)
, we do the following.

Define
f^{\prime}=f-\mathrm{deg}_{E^{\prime}}
.
Note that for each
i\in[2k]
, the set
B
contains at most one vertex of label
i
.
Let
b,u\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2k}
be the vectors defined as follows: We define
b_{i}=f^{\prime}(u)
, for the unique vertex
u\in B
labeled
i
if such a vertex exists, or
b_{i}=0
otherwise. We define
u_{i}=|\{u\in U\colon\lab_{G^{\prime}}(u)=i\}|
, i.e.
u_{i}
is the number of unit components, whose single vertex of requirement one is labeled
i
. For each choice of vectors
b^{\prime}\leq b
and
u^{\prime}\leq u
, where
\leq
is a componentwise inequality, let
H
be the bipartite graph defined by
H=(B\cup[2k],E_{H})
, where
E_{H}=\{(v,i)\in B\times[2k]\colon(\lab_{G^{\prime}}(v),i)\in\alpha^{\prime}\}
. We define the capacity function
c:V(H)\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
as
c(v)=b^{\prime}(v)
for
v\in B
and
c(i)=u^{\prime}(i)
for
i\in[2k]
. The algorithms verifies whether
(H,c)
is a YES-instance of the
Bipartite
b
-Matching
problem.

If this is the case, let
g
be a solution of the
Bipartite
b
-Matching
instance
(H,c)
. We construct the graph
G^{\prime\prime}
from
G^{\prime}
as follows: we assign
g(v,i)
unit vertices of label
i
to the vertex
v
, in such a way that each unit vertex is assigned to at most one vertex of
B
. Let
M
be the set of edges consisting of a single edge between each such unit vertex and its assigned vertex
v
. We define
G^{\prime\prime}
as the graph resulting from
G^{\prime}
by adding
M
to it, and we define the mapping
f^{\prime\prime}:V(G^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
as
f^{\prime\prime}(v)=f^{\prime}(v)-\mathrm{deg}_{M}(v)
. Next, relabel the vertices of
G^{\prime\prime}
by changing the labels of vertices in
V(F_{2})
from
i
to
i-k
, thus obtaining a graph
G^{\prime\prime}_{\text{rel}}
with labels in
[k]
.

If
\repr(G^{\prime\prime}_{\text{rel}},f^{\prime\prime})
is a pattern with at most one zero vector, then we add this pattern to
T^{(1)}_{x}
.
Next, we define

T^{(2)}_{x}=\{\rho_{\beta}(A)\colon A\in T^{(1)}_{x}\}
.
Finally, we set
T_{x}=\bigcup_{A\in T^{(2)}_{x}}\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
. The algorithm outputs YES if
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle\in T_{r}
at the root node
r
, or outputs NO otherwise.

\blacktriangle

Now we show that the computed table entries are equivalent to the records at each node:

Lemma 45
.

It holds for each
x\in\mathcal{V}
that
T_{x}
is equivalent to the record
\mathcal{R}_{x}
, i.e.,
T_{x}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x}
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by induction over
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
x
be a leaf node corresponding to an operation
\bullet_{i}
, i.e.
G_{x}
consists of a single vertex
v
labeled
i
.
Hence, the set of all partial solutions at
x
is
\big\{(G_{x},\{v\mapsto t\})\colon t\in D(v)\big\}
. It follows that
\mathcal{R}_{x}=\{\langle t\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\colon t\in D(v)\}=T_{x}
.

For an inner node
x\in\mathcal{V}
with children
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, it holds by the induction hypothesis that
T_{x_{1}}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x_{1}}
and
T_{x_{2}}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x_{2}}
. Since
T_{x}
results from
T^{(2)}_{x}
by applying
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
on each of its patterns, and since the union operation preserves equivalence, it suffices by
Corollary

40
to show that
T_{x}^{(2)}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x}
.
Let
G_{x}^{\prime}=G_{x_{1}}\oplus_{\alpha}G_{x_{2}}
and let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{x}
be the record of
(G_{x}^{\prime},D_{x})
. Let
\alpha^{R}=\{(i,j)\in[k]^{2}\colon(j,i)\in\alpha\}
.

Claim 46
.

We have
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{x}\simeq T_{x}^{(1)}
.

{claimproof}

[Proof of
Claim

46
]
Let
R\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{x}
and let
(F,g)
be a partial solution of
(G_{x}^{\prime},D_{x})
such that
R=\repr(F,g)
.
Let
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
be a fixed forest that is
\tilde{\alpha}
-compatible with
R
(i.e. with the canonical fixed forest of
R
). By
Observation

15
, without loss of generality we may assume that
\tilde{F}
has labels in
\{2k+1,\dots,3k\}
and correspondingly
\tilde{\alpha}\subseteq\{2k+1,\dots,3k\}\times[k]
.
By
Lemma

18
,
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
is
\tilde{\alpha}
-compatible with
(F,g)
. Let
T
be the
\tilde{\alpha}
-spanning forest for
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
and
(F,g)
.
We aim to construct an entry in
T_{x}^{(1)}
that
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
is
\tilde{\alpha}
-compatible with.

Let
F_{1}=F\cap G_{x_{1}}
and
F_{2}=F\cap G_{x_{2}}
. we define the mappings
g_{1}:V_{x_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and
g_{2}:V_{x_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
with
g_{1}=g+\mathrm{deg}_{F}-\mathrm{deg}_{F_{1}}
,
g_{2}=g+\mathrm{deg}_{F}-\mathrm{deg}_{F_{2}}
, where we apply the sum pointwise, and we restrict the domains to
V_{x_{1}}
and
V_{x_{2}}
, respectively.
Note that
(F_{1},g_{1})
is a partial solution of
(G_{x_{1}},D_{x_{1}})
and
(F_{2},g_{2})
is a partial solution of
(G_{x_{2}},D_{x_{2}})
, so
\repr(F_{1},g_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{x_{1}}
and
\repr(F_{2},g_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{x_{2}}
.
Intuitively, our goal is to identify an entry of
P_{x}
that corresponds to
(F_{1},g_{1})
and
(F_{2},g_{2})
, and to provide a valid selection of edges between the corresponding forests resulting in a compatible pattern.

Let
H_{1}
be the labeled forest containing vertices
V(F_{2})\cup V(\tilde{F})
, and edges
\{uv\in E(T)\colon u\not\in V(F_{1}),\>v\not\in V(F_{1})\}
(i.e., the forest obtained by deleting
F_{1}
from
T
).
The graph
\hat{H}:=H_{1}\oplus_{\alpha^{R}\cup\tilde{\alpha}}F_{1}
has vertices
V(F_{2})\cup V(\tilde{F})\cup V(F_{1})
. Since the vertices of
F_{2}
only use labels in
[k]
, the vertices of
\tilde{F}
only use labels in
\{k+1,\dots,2k\}
, and since
\alpha^{R}
and
\tilde{\alpha}
are disjoint, the edge set of
\hat{H}
is obtained by taking the edges between
F_{2}
and
F_{1}
corresponding to
\alpha^{R}
and the edges between
\tilde{F}
and
F_{1}
corresponding to
\tilde{\alpha}
. Thus
T
is a spanning tree in
\hat{H}
.

Therefore,
(H_{1},(\mathrm{deg}_{T}-\mathrm{deg}_{F_{1}})\cup(\mathrm{deg}_{T}-\mathrm{deg}_{H_{1}}))
is
(\alpha^{R}\cup\tilde{\alpha})
-compatible with
(F_{1},g_{1})
.
Since
\repr(F_{1},g_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{x_{1}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{x_{1}}\simeq T_{x_{1}}
(by induction hypothesis), there is an element
A_{1}\in T_{x_{1}}
such that
(H_{1},(\mathrm{deg}_{T}-\mathrm{deg}_{F_{1}})\cup(\mathrm{deg}_{T}-\mathrm{deg}_{H_{1}}))
is
(\alpha^{R}\cup\tilde{\alpha})
-compatible with
(F_{A_{1}},f_{A_{1}})
. Let
T_{1}
be the corresponding
(\alpha^{R}\cup\tilde{\alpha})
-spanning tree.

Let
H_{2}
be the labeled forest defined by the vertex set
V(F_{A_{1}})\cup V(\tilde{F})
and the edges
\{uv\in E(T_{1})\colon u\not\in V(F_{2}),v\not\in V(F_{2})\}
(i.e., the forest obtained by deleting
F_{2}
from
T_{1}
). Analogously,
(H_{2},(\mathrm{deg}_{T_{1}}-\mathrm{deg}_{F_{2}})\cup(\mathrm{deg}_{T_{1}}-\mathrm{deg}_{H_{2}}))
is
(\alpha\cup\tilde{\alpha})
-compatible with
(F_{2},g_{2})
,
and there is an element
A_{2}\in T_{x_{2}}
such that
(H_{2},(\mathrm{deg}_{T_{1}}-\mathrm{deg}_{F_{2}})\cup(\mathrm{deg}_{T_{1}}-\mathrm{deg}_{H_{2}}))
is
(\alpha\cup\tilde{\alpha})
-compatible with
(F_{A_{2}},f_{A_{2}})
. Let
T_{2}
be the corresponding
(\alpha\cup\tilde{\alpha})
-spanning tree.

Now we obtained a pair
(A_{1},A_{2})\in P_{x}
, and it remains to describe the set of edges that need to be added.
Let
E_{\text{add}}=\{uv\in E(T_{2})\colon u\in V(F_{A_{1}}),v\in V(F_{A_{2}})\}
.
Note that the labels of endpoints of edges in
E_{\text{add}}
belong to
\alpha\cup\tilde{\alpha}
. However, the vertices of
F_{A_{1}}
and
F_{A_{2}}
do not have labels in
\{2k+1,\dots,3k\}
, so the endpoints of all edges in
E_{\text{add}}
have labels in
\alpha
, i.e.,
E_{\text{add}}\subseteq E(F_{A_{1}}\oplus_{\alpha}F_{A_{2}})\setminus(E(F_{A_{1}})\cup E(F_{A_{2}}))
. By construction,
T_{2}
witnesses that

(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})\text{ is }\tilde{\alpha}\text{-compatible with }(F_{A_{1}}\cup F_{A_{2}}\cup E_{\text{add}},(f_{A_{1}}\cup f_{A_{2}})-\mathrm{deg}_{E_{\text{add}}}).

(*)

Let
F_{1}^{\prime}=F_{A_{1}}
and
F_{2}^{\prime}
be the forest obtained from
F_{A_{2}}
by relabelling each vertex of label
i
to
k+i
. Since
F_{A_{2}}
and
F_{2}^{\prime}
have the same underlying vertex set, we can regard
E_{\text{add}}
as edges between
F_{1}^{\prime}
and
F_{2}^{\prime}
.
Set
f_{1}=f_{A_{1}}
and
f_{2}(i+k)=f_{A_{2}}(i)
for
i\in[k]
.

Let
\hat{G}
be the disjoint union of
F_{1}^{\prime}
and
F_{2}^{\prime}
and
B
the set of vertices with non-zero requirement of the union of all big components of
\hat{G}
and
U
the set of vertices of non-zero requirement of the union of all unit components.
Since
E_{\text{add}}
is a subgraph of a tree, it is acyclic. In particular, since
|B|\leq 2k
,
E_{\text{add}}
contains at most
2k-1
edges between vertices of
B
. Additionally,
E_{\text{add}}
contains at most one edge between unit components of
\hat{G}
(otherwise the connectivity constraints would not be satisfied). It remains to show that the edges in
E_{\text{add}}
between big and unit components correspond to a solution of a
Bipartite
b
-Matching
instance.

Let
E_{UB}\subseteq E_{\text{add}}
be the set of edges between unit and big components of
\hat{G}
. Consider the
Bipartite
b
-Matching
instance with vertices
B\cup[2k]
, edges
E_{M}=\{(v,i)\in B\times[2k]\colon(\lab_{\hat{G}}(v),i)\in\alpha_{\text{rel}}\}
and capacities
c(v)=\mathrm{deg}_{E_{UB}}(v)
for
v\in B
and
c(i)=|\{u\in U\colon\lab_{\hat{G}}(u)=i,\mathrm{deg}_{E_{UB}}(u)=1\}|
. Note that the algorithm will, in some iteration, solve this instance of
Bipartite
b
-Matching
.

Using
E_{UB}
, we will construct a solution to this instance as follows. Let
m(v,i)=|\{u\in U\colon\lab_{\hat{G}}(u)=i,uv\in E_{UB}\}|
for all
v\in B,i\in[2k]
. Now for each
v\in B
, the capacity constraint is satisfied as
\sum_{iv\in E_{M}}m(v,i)=|\{u\in U\colon uv\in E_{UB}\}|=\mathrm{deg}_{E_{UB}}(v)
. Similarly, for
i\in[2k]
,
\sum_{iv\in E_{M}}m(v,i)=|\{uv\in E_{UB}\colon\lab_{\hat{G}}(u)=i\}|=c(i)
as each vertex in
U
has degree zero or one in
E_{UB}
.

Let
m^{\prime}
be the solution to this
Bipartite
b
-Matching
instance obtained by the algorithm and
M
the corresponding set of edges constructed by the algorithm. Consider the pattern added to
T_{x}^{(1)}
in this step.
For each vertex
v\in B
, its original requirement,
(f_{1}\cup f_{2})(v)
, will decrease by
|\{vv^{\prime}\in E_{\text{add}}\colon v^{\prime}\in B\}|+\mathrm{deg}_{M}(v)=|\{vv^{\prime}\in E_{\text{add}}\colon v^{\prime}\in B\}|+c(v)
. For each
i\in[2k]
, the pattern will have
c(i)
zero vectors, and the remaining
|\{u\in U\colon\lab_{\hat{G}}(u)=i,\mathrm{deg}_{E_{UB}}=0\}|
unit vectors will remain unchanged. Thus the pattern constructed in this iteration is equal to the pattern of
(\hat{G}\cup E_{\text{add}},(f_{1}\cup f_{2})-\mathrm{deg}_{E_{\text{add}}})
. By
Equation

*
and
Observation

15
,
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
and
(\hat{G}\cup E_{\text{add}},(f_{1}\cup f_{2})-\mathrm{deg}_{E_{\text{add}}})
are
\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
-compatible, where
\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}=\tilde{\alpha}\cup\{(i,j+k)\colon(i,j)\in\tilde{\alpha}\}
. Therefore, after relabelling, we have obtained a pattern in
T_{x}^{(1)}
that is
\tilde{\alpha}
-compatible with
(\tilde{F},\tilde{g})
.

The proof in the other direction can be obtained by reversing the direction of implications.

Claim 47
.

We have
\mathcal{R}_{x}\simeq T_{x}^{(2)}
.

{claimproof}

[Proof of
Claim

47
]
Let
R\in\mathcal{R}_{x}
be a pattern and let
(H,h)
be a partial solution with
\repr(H,h)=R
. Let
\alpha\subseteq[k]^{2}
and let
(F,g)
be a fixed forest that is
\alpha
-compatible with
(H,h)
and let
T
be the corresponding
\alpha
-spanning tree. We aim to construct an element of
T_{x}^{(2)}
such that
(F,g)
is
\alpha
-compatible with its canonical fixed forest.

Let
(H_{\varphi},h)
be the partial solution whose pattern belongs to
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{x}
, such that
H
is obtained from
H_{\varphi}
by applying the relabeling mapping
\beta
to
H_{\varphi}
.
Let
\alpha^{\prime}=\{(i,j):\>(i,\beta(j))\in\alpha\}
. Note that
F\oplus_{\alpha^{\prime}}H_{\varphi}
has the same edge set as
F\oplus_{\alpha}H
, so

(F,g)
is
\alpha^{\prime}
-compatible with
(H_{\varphi},h)
(with
\alpha^{\prime}
-spanning tree
T
). Since
\repr(H_{\varphi},h)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{x}
, and since it holds by
Claim

46
that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{x}\simeq T_{x}^{(1)}
, there must exist a pattern
A\in T_{x}^{(1)}
such that
(F,g)
is
\alpha^{\prime}
-compatible with
(F_{A},f_{A})
. Let
T_{1}
be the corresponding
\alpha^{\prime}
-spanning tree. Let
B=\rho_{\beta}(A)
.
Note that
B\in T_{x}^{(2)}
. It remains to prove that
(F,g)
is
\alpha
-compatible with
B
. Note that
F_{B}
is the same labeled forest as
F_{A}
, since
\rho_{\beta}
only affects the requirement function.
Let us construct an
\alpha
-spanning tree
T_{2}
in
F\oplus_{\alpha}F_{A}
as follows. For each edge
uv\in T_{1}
, where
u\in V(F)
and
v\in V(F_{A})
, we do the following. Let
\lab_{F_{A}}(v)=i
and let
C
be the connected component of
v
in
F_{A}
. We add to
T_{2}
the edge between
u
and the unique vertex in
C
with label
\beta(i)
. Note that, since the edge
uv
had labels
(\lab_{F}(u),i)\in\alpha^{\prime}
, the newly constructed edge of
T_{2}
has labels
(\lab_{F}(u),\beta(i))\in\alpha
.
Finally, we add to
T_{2}
all edges inside
F
and
F_{A}
. It is easy to see that
T_{2}
is a spanning tree in
F\oplus_{\alpha}F_{A}
. For each vertex
u\in V(F)
,
\mathrm{deg}_{T_{2}}(u)=\mathrm{deg}_{T_{1}}(u)
, and for each vertex
v\in V(F_{A})
,
\mathrm{deg}_{T_{2}}(v)
is equal to the sum of
\mathrm{deg}_{T_{1}}(v^{\prime})
, where
v^{\prime}
is a vertex in the same connected component as
v
and
\lab_{F_{A}}(v^{\prime})\in\beta^{-1}(\lab_{F_{A}}(v))
. Thus
(F,g)
is
\alpha
-compatible with
B
.

The other direction can be obtained by reversing the direction of implications.

Lastly,
Claim

47
and
Corollary

40
imply that
T_{x}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x}
, as desired.
∎

The following corollary tells us that the input is a YES-instance if and only if the table entry at the root contains the pattern
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle
. Informally, the pattern
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle
corresponds to a partial solution with one connected component, where all degree constraints are satisfied, i.e., a solution.

Corollary 48
.

The instance
(G,D)
is a YES-instance if and only if
T_{r}
contains
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle
is the only pattern that is
\emptyset
-compatible with the empty graph.

Suppose
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle\in T_{r}
. Since
T_{r}\simeq R_{r}
and
\mathbf{0}
is
\emptyset
-compatible with the empty graph, there is an element in
\mathcal{R}_{r}
that is
\emptyset
-compatible with the empty graph, i.e.,
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle\in\mathcal{R}_{r}
. The partial solution that corresponds to
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle
is a forest in
G
with one connected component that satisfies all degree requirements, i.e., it is a solution to our input instance.

For the other direction, let
T
be a spanning tree in
G
that satisfies all degree requirements. Then
\repr(T,\mathbf{0})=\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle\in\mathcal{R}_{r}
. By the same argument as above,
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle\in T_{r}
.
∎

Finally, we bound the running time of our algorithm:

Lemma 49
.

Algorithm
1
runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Proof.

For a join node
x\in\mathcal{V}
, let
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
be its children.
We claim that we can compute
T_{x}
in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
given
T_{x_{1}}
and
T_{x_{2}}
.

Firstly, by construction and
Corollary

40
,
T_{x_{1}}
and
T_{x_{2}}
are sets of nice patterns.
By Observation
21
, this implies that
|T_{x_{1}}|,|T_{x_{2}}|\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
Therefore,
|P_{x}|\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Secondly, we bound the complexity of computing
T^{(1)}_{x}
.
Since
|B|\leq 2k
and there are at most
n
unit components, the set
E^{\prime}
can be chosen in
n^{2}\cdot{4k^{2}\choose 2k-1}=n^{2}\cdot k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. Since
u
and
b
are
2k
-dimensional vectors, there are
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
ways to choose
u^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
. The
Bipartite
b
-Matching
problem is polynomial time solvable. Thus computing
T^{(1)}_{x}
takes
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
time.

Thirdly, since the relabeling operation does not increase the number of vectors, we have
|T^{(2)}_{x}|\leq|T^{(1)}_{x}|
.
Finally, to obtain
T_{x}
, we perform
|T^{(2)}_{x}|
calls of
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
, each of which takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, by
Corollary

42
.
Hence, in total, this step takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
The lemma then follows.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the main theorem for clique-width.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Given an instance
(G,D)
of
Set of Degrees MST
together with an NLC-decomposition of width
k
, we run Algorithm
1
. By Corollary
48
, the input instance is a YES-instance if and only if the table entry at the root contains the pattern
\langle\mathbf{0}\rangle
. The total running time is
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, by Lemma
49
.
∎

5
Upper Bound Techniques

Except for our clique-width algorithm, all our upper bounds rely on the Cut&Count technique that counts the number of weighted solutions (modulo two) in the given running time. We make use of the isolation lemma to reduce the decision version of this problem to the counting (modulo two) version with high probability.

Cut and Count.

Let
(G=(V,E),w,D,B)
be a weighted
Set of Degrees MST
instance, with
n=|V|
and
m=|E|
.
Further, let
d(v)=\max D(v)
for each vertex
v\in V
, and let
r=\max_{v\in V}d(v)
be the maximum requirement over all vertices.
Next, let
W=\max_{e\in E}w(e)
,
\overline{W}=[(n-1)\cdot W]_{0}
, and
\overline{M}=[m]_{0}
.
We will later fix a value
Z\in\mathbb{N}
that is bounded polynomially in
m
, and a second weight function
w^{\prime}:E\rightarrow[Z]_{0}
.
We set
\overline{Z}=[(n-1)\cdot Z]_{0}
.
Similar to
[
17
]
, we define the family of all solutions, relaxed solutions, and consistent cuts.

A
solution
is a set of edges
F\subseteq E
, such that
G[F]
is a tree, and
\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)\in D(v)
for each
v\in V
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the family of all solutions. We define families of weighted solutions, where for
\omega_{1}\in\overline{W}
and
\omega_{2}\in\overline{Z}
we define:

\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\big\{F\in\mathcal{S}\colon w(F)=\omega_{1},w^{\prime}(F)=\omega_{2}\big\}
.
Our goal is to compute the parity of
\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
for all values
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
. We achieve this by counting consistent cuts (defined below) using the Cut&Count technique.

We define a
relaxed solution
as a set of edges
F\subseteq E
where
|F|=n-1
, and for each vertex
v
it holds that
\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)\in D(v)
.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be the family of all relaxed solutions.
Similarly, we define families of weighted relaxed solutions:

\mathcal{R}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\big\{F\in\mathcal{R}\colon w(F)=\omega_{1},w^{\prime}(F)=\omega_{2}\}
.

Definition 50
.

Given a set of edges
F\in\mathcal{R}
, a
consistent cut
of
G[F]
is a pair
(F,c)
where
c:V\rightarrow\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}
is a mapping such that for each connected component
C\in{\mathsf{cc}}(G[F])
it holds that
|c(C)|=1
. For a relaxed solution
F\in\mathcal{R}
, let
\mathcal{C}(F)
be the family of all consistent cuts of
F
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the family of all consistent cuts of all relaxed solutions. We define families of consistent cuts of weighted relaxed solutions
\mathcal{C}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
as follows:

\mathcal{C}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\big\{(F,c)\in\mathcal{R}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\times\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}^{V}\colon(F,c)\text{ is a consistent cut of }G[F]\big\}.

Furthermore, let
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\big|\mathcal{C}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\big|
.

We use a slightly modified version of the Cut&Count technique that was first used by Hegerfeld and Kratsch
[
35
]
. They prove the following result:

Lemma 51

(
[
35
]
)
.

It holds that
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\equiv_{4}2\cdot\big|\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\big|
.

Proof.

It holds for each
F\in\mathcal{R}
that if
G[F]
is connected, then
G[F]
is acyclic as well, since
|F|=n-1
. Hence, it holds that
F\in\mathcal{S}
if and only if
F\in\mathcal{R}
and
G[F]
is connected.

Let
F\in\mathcal{R}
. Since all vertices of each connected component must be assigned one of the two values
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{R}
, and since we can choose this value independently for each connected component, it holds that
|\mathcal{C}(F)|=2^{|{\mathsf{cc}}(G[F])|}
. Therefore, it follows that

C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\mathcal{R}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\\
G[F]\text{ is connected}\end{subarray}}}2+{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\mathcal{R}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\\
G[F]\text{ is disconnected}\end{subarray}}}2^{|{\mathsf{cc}}(G[F])|}\equiv_{4}2\cdot\big|\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\big|,

where the congruence holds, since the second summand is always congruent to zero modulo four, as
G[F]
contains at least two connected components whenever
G[F]
is disconnected.
∎

In other words, it holds that
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
is always even, and that
\big|\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\big|
is odd, if and only if
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\equiv_{4}2
(i.e., if
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]/2
is odd). Hence, one can decide the parity of
|\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]|
by computing
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
modulo 4. We use the isolation lemma to show that this suffices to solve the decision version with high probability.

Isolation Lemma.

We say that a function
\omega:U\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

isolates
a set family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq 2^{U}
if there exists a unique
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
with
\omega(S^{\prime})=\min_{S\in\mathcal{F}}\omega(S)
.
Mulmuley et al.
[
46
]
showed that one can isolate any set family with high probability by choosing the weight function uniformly at random in a large enough space.

Lemma 52

(
[
46
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq 2^{U}
be a set family over a universe
U
with
|\mathcal{F}|>0
, and let
N>|U|
be an integer. For each
u\in U
, choose a weight
\omega(u)\in\{1,2,\dots,N\}
uniformly and independently at random. Then it holds that
P[\omega\text{ isolates }\mathcal{F}]\geq 1-|U|/N
.

Based on their result, we prove the following lemma:

Lemma 53
.

Let
Z=2\cdot m
. We choose
w^{\prime}:E\rightarrow[Z]_{0}
, where we choose
w^{\prime}(e)
independently and uniformly at random for each edge
e\in E
. Let
\omega_{1}\in\overline{W}
be the smallest weight of a solution
F\in\mathcal{S}
. Then with probability at least
1/2
, there exists a value
\omega_{2}\in\overline{Z}
such that
\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
contains a single solution only.

Proof.

Let
U=E
, and
\mathcal{F}=\{F\in\mathcal{S}\colon w(F)=\omega_{1}\}
. By
Lemma

52
, it holds that
P[\omega\text{ isolates }\mathcal{F}]\geq 1-|E|/Z=1/2
.
∎

In our remaining algorithmic results, we show how to compute the values
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
(modulo
4
) in the given running time.

6
Treewidth

Since our lower bound for treewidth follows directly from the one for pathwidth, our aim in this section is to obtain the following algorithmic upper bound for
Set of Degrees MST
:

See
5

Towards establishing Theorem
5
, let
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{B})
be a nice tree decomposition of
G
of width
\tw
rooted at
r
.
Intuitively, our algorithm uses records to store the following information about each vertex
v
in the bag: on which side of the consistent cut it lies (
L
or
R
; see Definition
50
) and the degree of
v
that has been used up so far. Our aim is to compute the size of the weighted families
\mathcal{C}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
modulo
4
in order to apply Lemma
51
.
At its core, we can formalize it as follows:

Algorithm 2
.

For a vertex set
S
, we call a mapping
c:S\rightarrow\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}
a
coloring
.
For
x\in\mathcal{V}
, we define the family of indices at
x
as

\mathcal{I}_{x}=([r]_{0})^{B_{x}}\times\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}^{B_{x}}
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{V}
, and let
x^{\prime}
be the child of
x
if
x
has a single child, or
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
be the children of
x
if it has two.
For
(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{M}\times\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
,
we define the tables
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{\mathcal{I}_{x}}
, where for
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
we define the value of
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
as follows:

•

Leaf node introducing vertex
v
:
Let
\phi
be the pair
(\emptyset_{v},\emptyset_{c})
, where
\emptyset_{v}
is a vector over an empty set, and
\emptyset_{c}
is the empty coloring.
We define
T_{x}[0,0,0][\phi]=1
, and
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=0
for all other values
a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},f,c
.

•

Introduce vertex
v
: Let
f^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}
be the restrictions of
f
and
c
to
B_{x^{\prime}}
. We define

T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=\begin{cases}T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]&\text{if }f(v)=0,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

Introduce edge
e=\{u,v\}
: Let
f^{\prime}
be the function resulting from
f
by decreasing
f(u)
and
f(v)
by one. Let
\omega_{1}^{\prime}=\omega_{1}-w(e)
and
\omega_{2}^{\prime}=\omega_{2}-w^{\prime}(e)
. We define

T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=\begin{cases}\!\begin{aligned} T_{x^{\prime}}&[a-1,\omega_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime}][f^{\prime},c]\\
&+T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\end{aligned}&\text{if }c(u)=c(v)\land f(u),f(v)>0,\\
T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

Forget vertex
v
: Let

T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in D(v)\\
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}\end{subarray}}}T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]].

•

Join node: Let

T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{1}+a^{2}=a\\
\omega_{1}^{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}=\omega_{1}\\
\omega_{2}^{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}=\omega_{2}\end{subarray}}}\sum\limits_{f^{1}+f^{2}=f}T_{x_{1}}[a^{1},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{2}^{1}][f^{1},c]\cdot T_{x_{2}}[a^{2},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{2}][f^{2},c].\penalty\hbox{}\penalty\hfill\quad\hbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\blacktriangle$}}

We formalize the intuition about the records via the following definition, and show that the two notions match in Lemma
55
.

Definition 54
.

For
x\in\mathcal{V}
,
(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{M}\times\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
, and
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
, we define the sets
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
as follows:

\displaystyle A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=\{(F,z)\in

\displaystyle 2^{E_{x}}\times\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}^{V_{x}}\colon

\displaystyle|F|=a\ \land w(F)=\omega_{1}\ \land w^{\prime}(F)=\omega_{2}\ \land

\displaystyle z|_{B_{x}}=c|_{B_{x}}\ \land

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)\in D(v)\text{ for all }v\in V_{x}\setminus B_{x}\ \land

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)=f(v)\text{ for all }v\in B_{x}\ \land

\displaystyle z\text{ is a consistent coloring of }V_{x}\}.

Lemma 55
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{V}
. It holds for all
(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{M}\times\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
,
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
that

T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction over
\mathcal{T}
. The base case is a leaf node
x
, where the claim holds by definition, since
G_{x}
is an empty graph. Let
x
be an internal node, and assume that the claim holds for all children of
x
. We show that the claim holds for
x
by distinguishing the different kinds of nodes
x
. Let
x^{\prime}
be the child of
x
if
x
has a single child, and
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
be the children of
x
otherwise.

•

Introduce vertex
v
: It holds that
\mathrm{deg}_{x}(v)=0
, and hence, for
f(v)\neq 0
it holds that
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=\emptyset
. Otherwise, let
f^{\prime}
be the restriction of
f
to
B_{x^{\prime}}
, and
c^{\prime}
be the restriction of
c
to
B_{x^{\prime}}
. Then one can define a bijection between
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
and
A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]
by assigning to a pair
(F,z)
the pair
(F,z^{\prime})
, where
z^{\prime}
is the restriction of
z
to
B_{x}
. Hence, it holds that

T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|=\big|A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]\big|\equiv_{4}T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}].

•

Introduce edge
e=\{u,v\}
: If
c(u)
and
c(v)
are not consistent or
f(u)=0
or
f(v)=0
, then it holds for all
(F,z)\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
that
e\notin F
. It follows in this case that
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=A_{x^{\prime}}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
. Assuming this is not the case, then it holds for each pair
(F,z)\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
that either
e\notin F
, and hence
(F,z)\in A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
, or
e\in F
, and
(F\setminus\{e\},z)\in A_{x^{\prime}}[a-1,\omega_{1}-w(e),\omega_{2}-w^{\prime}(e)][f^{\prime},c]
, where
f^{\prime}
is obtained from
f
by decreasing
f(u)
and
f(v)
by one.
Hence, it holds in this case that

\displaystyle T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|

\displaystyle=\big|A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|\dot{\cup}\big|A_{x^{\prime}}[a-1,\omega_{1}-w(e),\omega_{2}-w^{\prime}(e)][f^{\prime},c]\big|

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]+T_{x^{\prime}}[a-1,\omega_{1}-w(e),\omega_{2}-w^{\prime}(e)][f^{\prime},c].

•

Forget vertex
v
: The family
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
equals the disjoint union of all sets
A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]]
for all
o\in D(v)
and
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}
. This holds, since for each pair
(F,z)\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
, it must hold that
\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)\in D(v)
. Hence, for
o=\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)
, and
X=z(v)
, it holds that
(F,z)\in A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]]
. It follows that

\displaystyle T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|

\displaystyle={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in D(v)\\
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}\end{subarray}}}\big|A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]]\big|

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in D(v)\\
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}\end{subarray}}}T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]].

•

Join node: Let
a^{1},a^{2},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{1},\omega_{2}^{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a^{1}+a^{2}=a
,
\omega_{1}^{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}=\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}^{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}=\omega_{2}
.
Let
f_{1},f_{2}
be two vectors, such that
f_{1}+f_{2}=f
. Then for each pair
(F_{1},z_{1})\in A_{x_{1}}[a^{1},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{2}^{1}][f_{1},c]
and
(F_{2},z_{2})\in A_{x_{2}}[a^{2}\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{2}][f_{2},c]
it holds that

(F_{1}\dot{\cup}F_{2},z_{1}\cup z_{2})\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c].

Moreover, for each pair
(F,z)\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
there exists values
a^{1},a^{2},w_{1}^{1},w_{1}^{2},w_{2}^{1}+w_{2}^{2},F_{1},z_{1},F_{2},z_{2}
with

(F_{1},z_{1})\in A_{x_{1}}[a^{1},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{2}^{1}][f_{1},c]
,

(F_{2},z_{2})\in A_{x_{2}}[a^{2},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{2}][f_{2},c]
, such that
F=F_{1}\dot{\cup}F_{2}
and
z=z_{1}\cup z_{2}
. It follows that

\displaystyle T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|

\displaystyle={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{1}+a^{2}=a\\
w_{1}^{1}+w_{1}^{2}=\omega_{1}\\
w_{2}^{1}+w_{2}^{2}=\omega_{2}\end{subarray}}}\sum\limits_{f_{1}+f_{2}=f}\big|A_{x_{1}}[a^{1},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{2}^{1}][f_{1},c]\big|\cdot\big|A_{x_{2}}[a^{2},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{2}][f_{2},c]\big|

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{1}+a^{2}=a\\
w_{1}^{1}+w_{1}^{2}=\omega_{1}\\
w_{2}^{1}+w_{2}^{2}=\omega_{2}\end{subarray}}}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1}+f_{2}=f\end{subarray}}}T_{x_{1}}[a^{1},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{2}^{1}][f_{1},c]\cdot T_{x_{2}}[a^{2},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{2}][f_{2},c].

∎

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
55
, we obtain:

Lemma 56
.

For each
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
, it holds that
T_{r}[n-1,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][\phi]\equiv_{4}C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
.

Efficient Join Operations.

In order to obtain the desired running time, we first show how one can process a join node efficiently using fast convolution methods. We base our approach on the fast convolution technique introduced by van Rooij
[
52
]
that applies a multidimensional fast Fourier transformation to speed up the processing of join nodes. Instead of the usual trick of eliminating cyclic dependencies by doubling the underlying field, van Rooij applies filters over the total count of indices to cancel cyclic dependencies. We follow the same approach, in order to get the claimed running time.
We cite the following result from
[
52
]
with wording revised to our setting.

Definition 57

(Multidimensional Fast Fourier Transform)
.

Let
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
N=\mathbb{Z}_{h_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{Z}_{h_{k}}
.
For
f,f^{\prime}\in N
, we write
f+f^{\prime}
for the componentwise addition in
\mathbb{Z}_{h_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{Z}_{h_{n}}
, and we write
f\oplus f^{\prime}
for the componentwise addition in
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

Let
T_{1},T_{2}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{N}
be two tensors. We define the join operation
T_{1}\otimes T_{2}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{N}
, where for
x\in N
it holds that:

T_{1}\otimes T_{2}[x]={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b\in N\\
a+b=x\end{subarray}}}T_{1}[a]\cdot T_{2}[b].

Lemma 58

(
[
52
, Proposition 4]
)
.

Given two tensors
T_{1},T_{2}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{N}
, one can compute the tensor
T_{1}\otimes T_{2}
in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}(|N|\cdot\log|N|)
.

Based on this lemma, the authors of
[
52
]
show that one can compute acyclic convolution in the same running time (up to a polynomial factor), by filtering the indices by their total sum. This allows to cancel out cyclic dependencies without doubling the base of the exponent. We use the same technique to compute the join operation in our algorithm.

Lemma 59
.

Given two tensors
T_{1},T_{2}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{N}
, one can compute
in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}(|N|\cdot\log|N|)
the tensor
T\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{N}
defined by

T[f]=\sum\limits_{f_{1}\oplus f_{2}=f}T_{1}[f_{1}]\cdot T_{2}[f_{2}].

(1)

Proof.

Let
K=\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}
. For
\kappa\in[K]_{0}
, we define the tables
T_{1}^{\kappa},T_{2}^{\kappa}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}^{N}
, where for
x\in[2]
and
f\in N
it holds that

T_{x}^{\kappa}[f]=\begin{cases}T_{x}[f]&\text{if }\sum_{j=1}^{k}f_{j}=\kappa,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We define the table
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}^{N}
, where for
f\in N
and
\kappa=\sum_{j=1}^{k}f_{j}
it holds that

T^{\prime}[f]=\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sum\limits_{f_{1}+f_{2}=f}T_{1}^{j}[f_{1}]\cdot T_{2}^{\kappa-j}[f_{2}]=\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa}(T^{j}_{1}\otimes T^{\kappa-j}_{2})(f).

Clearly,
T^{\prime}[f]
can be computed by
\kappa+1
applications of the Multidimensional Fast Fourier Transform, and hence, by
Lemma

58
, it can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}(|N|\cdot\log|N|)
.

We claim that
T^{\prime}=T
. To see this, let
T_{1}[f_{1}]\cdot T_{2}[f_{2}]
be a summand of
Equation

1
. Then it must hold for
k_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(f_{1})_{j}
,
k_{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(f_{2})_{j}
and
\kappa=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(f)_{j}
that
k_{1}+k_{2}=\kappa
. Hence, it holds that
T^{\prime}[f]
contains the summand
T_{1}^{k_{1}}[f_{1}]\cdot T_{2}^{k_{2}}[f_{2}]
. For the other direction, let
f_{1},f_{2},k_{1},k_{2}
be such that
T_{1}^{k_{1}}[f_{1}]\neq 0
,
T_{2}^{k_{2}}[f_{2}]\neq 0
and
T_{1}^{k_{1}}[f_{1}]\cdot T_{2}^{k_{2}}[f_{2}]
appears in the sum of
T^{\prime}[f]
. Then it holds that
k_{1}+k_{2}=\kappa
. Moreover, if there exists some index
j\in[k]
such that
(f_{1})_{j}+(f_{2})_{j}>f_{j}
, then it must hold by the total sum, that there exists some index
j^{\prime}\in[k]
such that
(f_{1})_{j^{\prime}}+(f_{2})_{j^{\prime}}<f_{j^{\prime}}
, and hence
(f_{1})_{j^{\prime}}+(f_{2})_{j^{\prime}}\not\equiv_{\mathbb{Z}_{j^{\prime}}}f_{j^{\prime}}
, which contradicts the assumption, that this summand appears in the sum of
T^{\prime}[f]
. Hence, it must hold for each
j\in[k]
that
(f_{1})_{j}+(f_{2})_{j}=f_{j}
, and hence
f_{1}\oplus f_{2}=f
. It follows that
T^{\prime}[f]
contains the summand
T_{1}[f_{1}]\cdot T_{2}[f_{2}]
. This shows that
T^{\prime}=T
.
∎

Now we show how to compute the tables at a join node in the claimed running time:

Lemma 60
.

Let
x
be a join node, and let
a\in\overline{M}
,
\omega_{1}\in\overline{W}
, and
\omega_{2}\in\overline{Z}
. Then the table
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
can be computed
in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}\big((2r+2)^{\tw}\big)
.

Proof.

The algorithm iterates over all values
a^{1},a^{2},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{1},\omega_{2}^{2}
such that
a^{1}+a^{2}=a
,
\omega_{1}^{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}=\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}^{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}=\omega_{2}
. For each such tuple, and for each
c\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}^{B_{x}}
, let
T_{1}^{c}[f]=T_{x_{1}}[a^{1},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{2}^{1}][f,c]
and
T_{2}^{c}[f]=T_{x_{2}}[a^{2},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{2}][f,c]
. The algorithm computes the table
T^{c}=T_{1}^{c}\otimes T_{2}^{c}
. Let
T^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{\mathcal{I}_{x}}
with
T^{\prime}[f,c]=T^{c}[f]
. The algorithm adds the table
T^{\prime}
componentwise to
T_{x}
.

By definition, it holds that
T_{x_{1}}[a^{1},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{2}^{1}][f_{1},c_{1}]\cdot T_{x_{2}}[a^{2},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{2}][f_{2},c_{2}]
appears in the sum of
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
if and only if it holds that
a^{1}+a^{2}=a
,
\omega_{1}^{1}+\omega_{1}^{2}=\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}^{1}+\omega_{2}^{2}=\omega_{2}
,
c_{1}=c_{2}=c
and
f_{1}\oplus f_{2}=f
. Hence, the algorithm computes the correct table
T_{x}
.

The algorithm iterates over a polynomial number of tuples
a^{1},a^{2},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{1},\omega_{2}^{2}
, and over
2^{\tw+1}
mappings
c
, and for each the algorithm computes the table
T^{c}
in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}\big((r+1)^{\tw}\big)
by Lemma
59
. This concludes the proof. Hence, the algorithm runs in time

\mathcal{O}^{*}\big(2^{\tw+1}(r+1)^{\tw}\big)=\mathcal{O}^{*}\big((2r+2)^{\tw}\big)
.
∎

With Lemma
60
in hand, we can obtain an upper bound on dynamically computing the tables at each node of
\mathcal{T}
and subsequently use this to establish the main result of this section:

Lemma 61
.

All tables
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}((2r+2)^{\tw}\cdot W^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot Z^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
.

Proof.

The algorithm computes all tables
T_{x}
by dynamic programming in a bottom up manner over the decomposition tree. For each node
x
, the algorithm iterates over all values
(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{M}\times\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
, and for each such tuple the algorithm computes
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
as follows: let
x^{\prime}
be the child of
x
if
x
has a single child or
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
be the children of
x
if it has two children.

•

For a leaf node
x
, the algorithm initializes
T_{x}[0,0,0][\phi]=1
and
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=0
for all other values
a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},f,c
.

•

Introduce vertex node
(v)
, the algorithm iterates over all indices
(f^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}_{x^{\prime}}
. For each the algorithm sets
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]
where
f^{\prime}=f[v\mapsto 0]
for both extensions
c^{\prime}
of
c
by
v\mapsto\boldsymbol{L}
and
v\mapsto\boldsymbol{R}
. The algorithm sets
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=0
for all other values
f,c
.

•

Introduce edge
e=\{u,v\}
: the algorithm iterates over all indices
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x^{\prime}}
. For each the algorithm sets
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
. After that, for each coloring
c
such that
c(u)=c(v)
and
f(u),f(v)<d
, the algorithm adds
T_{x^{\prime}}[a-1,\omega_{1}-w(e),\omega_{2}-w^{\prime}(e)][f,c]
to
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c]
where
f^{\prime}=f[u\mapsto f(u)+1,v\mapsto f(v)+1]
.

•

Forget vertex
v
: the algorithm iterates over all indices
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
. For each the algorithm sets
T_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=0
. After that the algorithm iterates over all indices
(f^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}_{x^{\prime}}
. For each index
f^{\prime}
with
f^{\prime}(v)\in D(v)
, the algorithm adds the value of
T_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]
to
T_{x}[f,c]
where
f
is the restriction of
f^{\prime}
to
B_{x}
and
c
is the restriction of
c^{\prime}
to
B_{x}
.

•

Join node: This follows by
Lemma

60
by iterating over all values
a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
.

Clearly, the number of values
a^{1},a^{2},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{1},\omega_{2}^{2}
the algorithm iterates over for each node
x
is polynomial in
n
,
W
and
Z
. For a leaf node, an introduce vertex, introduce edge or forget vertex node, the algorithm iterates over each index of
\mathcal{I}_{x}
and
\mathcal{I}_{x^{\prime}}
at most once, and for each index the algorithm performs a constant number of operations. Hence, all these nodes can be processed in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}((2r+2)^{\tw})
.

For a join node, each table
T_{a^{1},a^{2},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{1},\omega_{2}^{2}}
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}((2r+2)^{\tw})
by
Lemma

60
. Since the total number of tuples
(a^{1},a^{2},\omega_{1}^{1},\omega_{1}^{2},\omega_{2}^{1},\omega_{2}^{2})
is polynomial in
n
,
W
and
Z
, the algorithm computes all tables
T_{x}
in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}((2r+2)^{\tw}\cdot W^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot Z^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5
.

The algorithm fixes
Z=2m=n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and computes all tables
T_{x}
for all nodes
x
in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}((2r+2)^{\tw}\cdot W^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
by
Lemma

61
.
The algorithm accepts, if there exists a value
\omega_{2}\in\overline{Z}
such that
T_{r}[n-1,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][\phi]=2
and rejects otherwise.

It holds by
Lemma

53
that with probability at least
1/2
there exists a value
\omega_{2}\in\overline{Z}
such that
\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
contains a single solution only, if a solution exists, and none otherwise. It holds by
Lemma

56
that
T_{r}[n-1,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][\phi]\equiv_{4}C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
, and by
Lemma

51
that
C[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\equiv_{4}2
if
\big|\mathcal{S}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\big|=1
. Hence, if a solution exists the algorithm accepts with probability at least
1/2
, and rejects otherwise.
∎

7
Pathwidth

In this section, we provide an upper bound that uses pathwidth to supersede the one obtained in Theorem
5
and also a corresponding lower bound.

7.1
The Upper Bound

Our aim here is to prove:
See
3

Let
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{B})
be a nice path decomposition of width
\pw
, and
r
the root of
\mathcal{T}
.
For a subgraph
H
of
G
, let
q_{H}(v)=\min\{\mathrm{deg}_{H}(v),D(v)\}
for each vertex
v\in V(H)
.
For
x\in\mathcal{V}
, let
q_{x}=q_{G_{x}}
.

Dynamic programming tables.

To obtain our tight runtime bound, we need to extend the notion of coloring as follows:

Definition 62
.

For a vertex set
S
, we call a mapping
c\colon S\rightarrow\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}
a
partial coloring
. We define the consistency relation
\sim
over
\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}
with
x\sim y
if and only if
\{x,y\}\neq\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}
. For a graph
H
, we call a partial coloring
c:V(H)\rightarrow\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}

consistent
, if it holds for each edge
\{u,v\}\in E(H)
that
c(u)\sim c(v)
.

The dynamic programming table at a node
x
is indexed by the family
\mathcal{I}_{x}
, defined below.

Definition 63
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{x}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{B_{x}}
be the family of all vectors that are upper bounded (componentwise) by
q_{x}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{D}_{x}=\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{B_{x}}\colon u_{v}\leq q_{x}(v)\text{ for all }v\in B_{x}\},

and let
\mathcal{C}_{x}=\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}^{B_{x}}
. We define

\mathcal{I}_{x}=\big\{(f,c)\in\mathcal{D}_{x}\times\mathcal{C}_{x}\colon c(v)=\uparrow\iff f_{v}\in\{0,d(v)\}\big\}.

The algorithm we use for pathwidth then proceeds essentially analogously as Algorithm
2
, with the following distinction: if a vertex
v
in the bag has degree
0
then we do not need to fix its side in the consistent cut, while if it has degree
r
then we need not store it. Implementing this change yields dynamic programming tables
\hat{T}
that require two fewer states per vertex, and this also directly translates into the running time since we do not deal with join nodes.

Formally, the tables are defined recursively over
\mathcal{T}
as follows:

Algorithm 64
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{V}
, and let
x^{\prime}
be the child of
x
(if exists). For
(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{M}\times\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
,
we define the tables
\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{\mathcal{I}_{x}}
, where for
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
we define the value of
\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
depending on the kind of node
x
as follows:

•

For a leaf node
x
,
let
\phi
be the pair
(\emptyset_{v},\emptyset_{c})
, where
\emptyset_{v}
is a the vector over an empty set, and
\emptyset_{c}
is the empty partial coloring.
It holds that
(f,c)=\phi
. We set
\hat{T}_{x}[0,0,0][f,c]=1
, and
\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=0
for all other values
a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
.

•

Introduce vertex
v
: let
f^{\prime},c^{\prime}
be the restrictions of
f
and
c
to
B_{x^{\prime}}
respectively. Since
q_{x}(v)\leq\mathrm{deg}_{x}(v)=0
, it must hold that
f(v)=0
and
c(v)=\uparrow
. We set

\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=\hat{T}_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}].

•

Forget vertex
v
:
We define

\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in D(v)\\
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}\\
X=\uparrow\iff o=d(v)\end{subarray}}}\hat{T}_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\big[f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]\big],

•

Introduce edge
e=\{u,v\}
:
If
f(u)=0
or
f(v)=0
, or
c(u)
and
c(v)
are not consistent, we set
\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=\hat{T}_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
. Assuming this is not the case, let
a^{\prime}=a-1
,
\omega^{\prime}_{1}=\omega_{1}-w(e)
and
\omega^{\prime}_{2}=\omega_{2}-w^{\prime}(e)
. Let
f^{\prime}=f[u\mapsto f_{u}-1,v\mapsto f_{v}-1]
. If
f_{u}=d_{u}
and
f_{v}=d_{v}
, we define
Q_{u}=Q_{v}=\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}
. Otherwise, it holds that the set
Q=\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}\cap\{f_{u},f_{v}\}
is a singleton. Let
Q_{u}=Q
if
f(u)\geq 2
or
Q_{u}=\{\uparrow\}
otherwise, and
Q_{v}=Q
if
f_{v}\geq 2
or
Q_{v}=\{\uparrow\}
otherwise. We set

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]

\displaystyle+{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{u}\in Q_{u},X_{v}\in Q_{v}\\
X_{u}\sim X_{v}\end{subarray}}}\hat{T}_{x^{\prime}}\big[a^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}_{1},\omega^{\prime}_{2}\big]\big[f^{\prime},c[u\mapsto X_{u},v\mapsto X_{v}]\big].\penalty\hbox{}\penalty\hfill\quad\hbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\blacktriangle$}}

Definition 65
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{V}
. For a mapping
f\colon B_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{x}
, we define the set
Q_{f}=\{v\in B_{x}\colon f(v)<d(v)\}
. For

(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{M}\times\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
, and
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
we define the sets
A_{x}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
as follows:

\displaystyle A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=\{(F,z)\in

\displaystyle 2^{E_{x}}\times\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}^{V_{x}}\colon

\displaystyle|F|=a,w(F)=\omega_{1},w^{\prime}(F)=\omega_{2}\ \land

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)\in D(v)\text{ for all }v\in V_{x}\setminus B_{x}\ \land

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)=f(v)\text{ for all }v\in B_{x}\ \land

\displaystyle z|_{Q_{x}}=c|_{Q_{f}}\ \land

\displaystyle z(v)\neq\uparrow\text{ for all }v\in V_{x}\setminus Q_{f}\ \land

\displaystyle z\text{ is a consistent partial coloring of }G_{x}\}.

Intuitively, the tables
A_{x}
store the set of all consistent partial colorings
z
of all partial solutions
F
in
G_{x}
that have the footprint
(f,c)
. In particular,
z
is an extension of
c|_{Q_{f}}
that assigns
\boldsymbol{L}
or
\boldsymbol{R}
to each vertex of
V_{x}\setminus Q_{f}
.

Lemma 66
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{V}
. It holds for all
(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\overline{M}\times\overline{W}\times\overline{Z}
,
(f,c)\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
that

\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction over
\mathcal{T}
. The base case is a leaf node
x
, where the claim holds by definition, since
G_{x}
is an empty graph. Let
x
be an internal node, and assume that the claim holds for
x^{\prime}
the child of
x
. We show that the claim holds for
x
by distinguishing the different kinds of nodes
x
.

•

Introduce vertex
v
: It holds that
\mathrm{deg}_{x}(v)=0
, and hence,
f(v)=0
and
c(v)=\uparrow
. Let
f^{\prime},c^{\prime}
be the restrictions of
f
and
c
to
B_{x^{\prime}}
respectively. Then there exists a bijection between
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
and
A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]
, by assigning to a pair
(F,z)\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]
the pair
(F,z^{\prime})\in A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
, where
z^{\prime}
is the restriction of
z
to
B_{x^{\prime}}
. Hence, it holds that

\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|=\big|A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}]\big|\equiv_{4}\hat{T}_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f^{\prime},c^{\prime}].

•

Forget vertex
v
: It holds that
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
equals the disjoint union of all sets
A_{x^{\prime}}\big[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\big]\big[f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]\big]
for all
o\in D(v)
and
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}
where
X=\uparrow
if and only if
o=d(v)
. This holds since for each pair
(F,z)\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
it must hold that
\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)\in D(v)
, and hence, for
o=\mathrm{deg}_{F}(v)
, and
X=z(v)
if
o<d(v)
or
X=\uparrow
otherwise, that
(F,z)\in A_{x^{\prime}}\big[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\big]\big[f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]\big]
. Hence, it holds that

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}\big|A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]\big|

\displaystyle={\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in D(v)\\
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}\\
o=d(v)\iff X=\uparrow\end{subarray}}}\big|A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]]\big|

\displaystyle\equiv_{4}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in D(v)\\
X\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R},\uparrow\}\\
o=d(v)\iff X=\uparrow\end{subarray}}}\hat{T}_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f[v\mapsto o],c[v\mapsto X]].

•

Introduce edge
e=\{u,v\}
: If
f(u)=0
or
f(v)=0
, or
c(u)
and
c(v)
are not consistent, then it holds for all
(F,z)\in A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
that
e\notin F
. It follows in this case that
A_{x}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]=A_{x^{\prime}}[a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}][f,c]
