and a vertex of label
j
in
F
is exactly
\sigma(i,j)
.
Therefore,
T
is a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F
which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 103
.

Let
\mathscr{O}\coloneq(\iso,O,b)
be an ordered pattern with
O=(u_{1},S_{1}),\dots,(u_{m},S_{m})
, and
i\in[m]
. For a bad coordinate
(q,X)\in Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i)
for
q\in[k]
and
X\in\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}
, let
\mathscr{O}^{2}=\pi_{2}(\mathscr{O},i,q,X)
.
Then it holds that
\mathscr{O}^{2}\preceq\mathscr{O}
.

Proof.

We follow the same approach as in the proof of
Lemma

102
.
In particular, let
\mathscr{O}^{2}=(\iso,O^{2},b^{2})
with
O^{2}=(u^{\prime}_{1},S^{\prime}_{m}),\dots(u^{\prime}_{m},S^{\prime}_{m})
. Let
j=b(q,X)
.
Let
F
and
F^{\prime}
be the corresponding fixed forests of
p(\mathscr{O})
and
p(\mathscr{O}^{2})
, respectively.
Then we define
C_{t},C^{\prime}_{t},T^{\prime},\bar{T}
the same way as in the proof of
Lemma

102
.
We now also aim to add some edges to
\bar{T}
to form a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F
.

Consider a connected component
C
of
\bar{T}
that is not
C_{i}
or
C_{j}
.
There are three cases.

Case 1.

C
is incident to only
C^{\prime}_{i}
in
T
.

In this case, there is a removed edge in the construction of
\bar{T}
that connects a vertex
w\in C
and a vertex
v^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}_{i}
.
By definition of
\pi_{2}
, regardless of
X=\mathbf{L}
or
X=\mathbf{I}
,
C_{i}
has a vertex
v
of the same type and label as
v^{\prime}
.
Then we add the edge
wv
to
\bar{T}
.

Case 2.

C
is incident to only
C^{\prime}_{j}
in
T
.

In this case, there is a removed edge in the construction of
\bar{T}
that connects a vertex
w\in C
and a vertex
v^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}_{j}
.
By definition of
\pi_{2}
and by the fact that
j=b(q,X)
, we can see that for every combination of type and label that appears in
C^{\prime}_{j}
, it also appears in
C_{j}
.
Hence, there is a vertex
v
in
C_{j}
with the same type and label as
v^{\prime}
.
Then we add the edge
wv
to
\bar{T}
.

Case 3.

C
is incident to both
C^{\prime}_{i}
and
C^{\prime}_{j}
in
T
.

Note that there is only one connected component in this case, since
T
is a tree.
There must be two removed edges, one between a vertex
w_{i}\in C
and a vertex
v^{\prime}_{i}\in C^{\prime}_{i}
and another between a vertex
w_{j}\in C
and a vertex
v^{\prime}_{j}\in C^{\prime}_{j}
.
Similar to the argument in Case 1,
C_{i}
has a vertex
v_{i}
with the same type and label as
v^{\prime}_{i}
.
Similar to the argument in Case 2,
C_{j}
has a vertex
v_{j}
with the same type and label as
v^{\prime}_{j}
.
Then we add the edges
v_{i}w_{i}
and
v_{j}w_{j}
to
\bar{T}
.

The argument for the resulting
T
to be a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F
is similar to the proof of
Lemma

102
.
∎

Lemma 104
.

Let
\mathscr{O}\coloneq(\iso,O,b)
be an ordered pattern with
O=(u_{1},S_{1}),\dots,(u_{m},S_{m})
, and
i\in[m]
. For a bad coordinate
(q,X)\in Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i)
for
q\in[k]
and
X\in\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}
, and let
\mathscr{O}^{1}=\pi_{1}(\mathscr{O},i,q,X)
and
\mathscr{O}^{2}=\pi_{2}(\mathscr{O},i,q,X)
.
Then it holds that
\{\mathscr{O}\}\preceq\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\mathscr{O}^{2}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{O}^{1}=(\iso,O^{1},b^{1})
with
O^{1}=(u^{1}_{1},S^{1}_{m}),\dots(u^{1}_{m},S^{1}_{m})
. Let
j=b(q,X)
.
Let
\mathscr{O}^{2}=(\iso,O^{2},b^{2})
with
O^{2}=(u^{2}_{1},S^{2}_{m}),\dots(u^{2}_{m},S^{2}_{m})
.
Let
F
,
F^{1}
, and
F^{2}
be the corresponding fixed forests of
p(\mathscr{O})
,
p(\mathscr{O}^{1})
, and
p(\mathscr{O}^{2})
, respectively.
For
t\in[m]
, let
C_{t}
be the tree of
F
corresponding to
(u_{t},S_{t})
.
We define
C^{1}_{t}
and
C^{2}_{t}
analogously for
F^{1}
and
F^{2}
.
Similar to the proof of
Lemma

102
, we consider
C_{t}
,
C^{1}_{t}
, and
C^{2}_{t}
to be identical for
t\in[m]\setminus\{i,j\}
.

For a mapping
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}
, an integer value
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and a fixed forest
F^{\prime\prime}
,
suppose there exists a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree
T
for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F
.
We need to construct a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree either for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{1}
or for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{2}
.
Let
T^{\prime}
be the forest resulting from removing all the vertices of
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
from
T
.
Let
C^{*}
be the unique connected component of
T^{\prime}
that is connected to
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
directly by an edge in
T
.
That is, there exist two edges in
T
, one connecting a vertex
w_{i}\in C^{*}
with a vertex
v_{i}\in C_{i}
and one connecting a vertex
w_{j}\in C^{*}
with a vertex
v_{j}\in C_{j}
.
We distinguish two cases depending on the type and label of
v_{i}
.
For a vertex
v
in
F
, we define the
label-type
of
v
as the pair
(q,X)
where
q=\lab_{F}(v)
, and
X=\mathbf{L}
if
v
is a leaf of
F
, or
X=\mathbf{I}
otherwise.

Case 1.
If the label-type of
v_{i}
is not equal to
(q,X)
:
Since
C^{2}_{i}
results from
C_{i}
by removing all vertices of the label-type
(q,X)
, and adding them to
C^{2}_{j}
(where we upper bound the multiplicity of inner vertices by one), it holds first that
C^{2}_{i}
has a vertex
v^{2}_{i}
of the same type and label as
v_{i}
, and
C^{2}_{j}
has a vertex
v^{2}_{j}
of the same type and label as
v_{j}
.
We then add the edges
v^{2}_{i}w_{i}
and
v^{2}_{j}w_{j}
to
T^{\prime}
.
Second, We can define an injective mapping between the vertices of
C_{j}
and the vertices of
C^{2}_{j}
that preserves both type and label. We can extend this mapping to cover the vertices of
C_{i}
of label-type
(q,X)
, where the restriction of the resulting mapping to leaves (on both sides) is even bijective. Let us extend this mapping by the identity over the rest of
T
. Then we can replace each edge between
F
and
F^{\prime\prime}
in
T
that is not incident to
C_{i}
or
C_{j}
with the edge between the images of its endpoints under this mapping. Intuitively, we have shifted all edges whose endpoints in
C_{j}
have label-type
(q,X)
to
C^{2}_{j}
.

By the same argument as in the end of the proof of
Lemma

102
, we obtain that the resulting graph is a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{2}
.

Case 2.
Otherwise, the vertex
v_{i}
has label-type
(q,X)
:
Since
C^{1}_{i}
results from
C_{i}
by removing all vertices but a single vertex of label-type
(q,X)
, and adding them to
C^{1}_{j}
, it holds first that
C^{1}_{i}

has a vertex
v^{1}_{i}
with the same type and label as
v_{i}
and
C^{1}_{j}
has a vertex
v^{1}_{j}
of the same label and type as
v_{j}
.
We then add the edges
v^{1}_{i}w_{i}
and
v^{1}_{j}w_{j}
to
T^{\prime}
.
With analogous arguments as in Case 1, we can
define a mapping between all vertices of
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
except for a single vertex of the label-type
(q,X)
and the vertices of
C^{1}_{j}
that preserve both type and label, and such that the restriction of this mapping to leaves is bijective. We extend this mapping by the identity over the rest of
T
.
We then replace each edge between
F
and
F^{\prime\prime}
in
T
that is not incident to
C_{i}
or
C_{j}
with the edge between the images of its endpoints under this mapping. Intuitively we shift all edges from
C_{i}
to
C_{j}
, except for the single edge tha connects them.
Again, we conclude that the resulting graph is a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{1}
.
Thus we obtain a
(\sigma,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{1}
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
102
,
103
and
104
immediately yields the following corollary:

Corollary 105
.

Let
\mathscr{O}\coloneq(\iso,O,b)
be an ordered pattern with
O=(u_{1},S_{1}),\dots,(u_{m},S_{m})
, an index
i\in[m]
and a coordinate
q\in[k]
and
X\in\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}
such that
(q,X)\in Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i)
.
Let
j=b(q,X)
.
Further, let
\mathscr{O}^{1}=\pi_{1}(\mathscr{O},i,q,X)
and
\mathscr{O}^{2}=\pi_{2}(\mathscr{O},i,q,X)
.
Then
\{\mathscr{O}\}\simeq\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\mathscr{O}^{2}\}
.

Subroutines.

Based on the functions
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, we formulate a subroutine that transforms a single pattern into an equivalent family of nice patterns.
The first two subroutines
\operatorname{ShiftVectorL}
and
\operatorname{ShiftVectorI}
take as input an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}\coloneq(\iso,O,b)
, and a bad index
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}}
, and output a set of ordered patterns, that result from an exhaustive application of the operations
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
to all bad coordinates of either type (
\mathbf{L}
or
\mathbf{I}
) of
i
. We attach to each resulting pattern a single index. This is the index we shifted to by an application of
\pi_{1}
, and is the only index that may have turned bad in the resulting pattern.

Procedure 5
.

The operation
\operatorname{ShiftVectorL}
takes as input a tuple
(\mathscr{O},i)
, where
\mathscr{O}\coloneq(\iso,O,b)
is an ordered pattern and
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}}^{\mathbf{L}}
.
Let
O=(u_{1},S_{1}),\dots(u_{m},S_{m})
.
Further, let
Z_{\mathscr{O}}^{\mathbf{L}}(i)=\{(z_{1},\mathbf{L}),\dots,(z_{t},\mathbf{L})\}
.
The operation outputs a set of pairs
\{(\mathscr{O}^{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{t+1},p_{t+1})\}
, where
p_{j}=b(z_{j},\mathbf{L})
for
j\in[t]
and
p_{t+1}=\perp
. Let
We define the pattern
\mathscr{O}^{j}=(\iso,O^{j},b^{j})
for
j\in[t+1]
with

O^{j}=(u^{j}_{1},S^{j}_{1}),\dots,(u^{j}_{m},S^{j}_{m})
as follows:

For
j\in[t]
and
d\in[m]
we define the vector
(u^{j}_{d},S^{j}_{d})
as follows:
If
d=i
, we define
(u^{j}_{d},S^{j}_{d})=(\mathbf{e}_{z_{j}},\emptyset)
.
If
d=b(z_{j},\mathbf{L})
, we define
S^{j}_{d}=S_{d}\cup S_{i}
and

(u^{j}_{d})_{z}=\begin{cases}(u_{d})_{z}&\colon z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1}\}\text{ and }d\neq b(z,\mathbf{L}),\\
(u_{d})_{z}+(u_{i})_{z}-1&\colon z=z_{j},\\
(u_{d})_{z}+(u_{i})_{z}&\colon\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Otherwise, we define
S^{j}_{d}=S_{d}
and

(u^{j}_{d})_{z}=\begin{cases}(u_{d})_{z}+(u_{i})_{z}&\colon z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1}\}\text{ and }d=b(z,\mathbf{L}),\\
(u_{d})_{z}&\colon\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We define
b^{j}
as the mapping such that
b^{j}(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})=b(z_{j},\mathbf{L})
for all values
(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})\in b^{-1}(i)
, and
b^{j}(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})=b(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})
for all other pairs
(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})\in[k]\times\{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{L}\}
.

Finally, we define
b^{t+1}=b
.
For
d\in[m]
, we define
S^{t+1}_{d}=S_{d}
and

(u^{t+1}_{d})_{z}=\begin{cases}0&\colon d=i\land(z,\mathbf{L})\in Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i),\\
(u_{j})_{z}+(u_{i})_{z}&\colon d=b(z,\mathbf{L})\land(z,\mathbf{L})\in Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i),\\
(u_{d})_{z}&\colon\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

\blacktriangle

Observation 106
.

Let
(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},p)
be some pair in the output of
\operatorname{ShiftVectorL}(\mathscr{O},i)
with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. If
p\neq perp
, then all following hold:

•

I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
or
I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=(I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\})\cup\{p\}
for each value
X\in\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}
, and

•

b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})=b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\setminus\{i\}
.

For
p=\perp
, the following points hold:

•

I^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
,

•

I^{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I^{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathscr{O}}
,

•

and
b^{\prime}=b
.

Procedure 6
.

The operation
\operatorname{ShiftVectorI}
takes as input a tuple
(\mathscr{O},i)
, where
\mathscr{O}\coloneq(\iso,O,b)
is an ordered pattern and
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}}^{\mathbf{I}}
.
Let
O=(u_{1},S_{1}),\dots(u_{m},S_{m})
.
Further, let
Z_{\mathscr{O}}^{\mathbf{I}}(i)=\{(z_{1},\mathbf{I}),\dots,(z_{t},\mathbf{I})\}
, where
z_{1}<\dots<z_{t}
.
The operation outputs a set of pairs
\{(\mathscr{O}^{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{t+1},p_{t+1})\}
, where
p_{j}=b(z_{j},\mathbf{I})
for
j\in[t]
and
p_{t+1}=\perp
. We define the patterns

\mathscr{O}^{j}=(\iso,O^{j},b^{j})
for
j\in[t+1]
with

O^{j}=(u^{j}_{1},S^{j}_{1}),\dots,(u^{j}_{m},S^{j}_{m})
as follows:

For
j\in[t]
and
d\in[m]
, we define

u^{j}_{d}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{0}&\colon d=i,\\
u_{d}+u_{i}&\colon d=b(z_{j},\mathbf{I}),\\
u_{d}&\colon\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad S^{j}_{d}=\begin{cases}\{z_{j}\}&\colon d=i,\\
S_{d}\cup(S_{i}\setminus\{z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1}\})&\colon d=b(z_{j},\mathbf{I}),\\
S_{d}&\colon\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We define
b^{j}
as the mapping such that
b^{j}(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})=b(z_{j},\mathbf{I})
for all values
(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})\in b^{-1}(i)
, and
b^{j}(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})=b(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})
for all other pairs
(z^{\prime},X^{\prime})\in[k]\times\{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{L}\}
.

Finally, we define
b^{t+1}=b
.
For
d\in[m]
, we define
u^{t+1}_{d}=u_{d}
and

S^{t+1}_{d}=\begin{cases}S_{d}\setminus\{z_{1},\dots,z_{t}\}&\colon d=i\\
S_{d}&\colon\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Observation 107
.

Let
(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},p)
be some pair in the output of
\operatorname{ShiftVectorI}(\mathscr{O},i)
with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. If
p\neq\perp
, then all of the following hold:

•

I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
or
I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=(I^{X}_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\})\cup\{p\}
for each value
X\in\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}
, and

•

b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})=b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\setminus\{i\}
.

For
p=\perp
, the following points hold:

•

I^{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I^{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
,

•

I^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathscr{O}}
,

•

and
b^{\prime}=b
.

Lemma 108
.

Given an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}
and an index
i\in I^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathscr{O}}
, let
P
be the set resulting from
\operatorname{ShiftVectorL}(\mathscr{O},i)
and let
S=\{\mathscr{O}\colon(\mathscr{O},p)\in P\}
. Then
S
is equivalent to
\mathscr{O}
. The same holds if
i\in I^{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathscr{O}}
and we use
\operatorname{ShiftVectorI}(\mathscr{O},i)
instead.

Proof.

We prove the claim for
\operatorname{ShiftVectorL}(\mathscr{O},i)
and
i\in I^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathscr{O}}
.
The proof for
\operatorname{ShiftVectorI}(\mathscr{O},i)
follows from the analogous arguments applied to
Z^{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathscr{O}}(i)
instead of
Z^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathscr{O}}(i)
.

Let
Z_{\mathscr{O}}^{\mathbf{L}}(i)=\{(z_{1},\mathbf{L}),\dots,(z_{t},\mathbf{L})\}
, and let
\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{t+1}
be the set of ordered patterns as defined in
Procedure

5
.
We define the sequence of ordered patterns
\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\dots,\gamma^{t}
with

\gamma^{0}=\mathscr{O}
, and
\gamma^{j}=\pi_{2}(\gamma^{j-1},i,z_{j},X)
for
j\in[t]
.
Then it holds for each
j\in[t]
that
\mathscr{O}^{j}=\pi_{1}(\gamma^{j-1},i,z_{j},X)
.
We show by induction over
j
that
\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{j}\}\cup\{\gamma^{j}\}
is equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}\}
.
The lemma then follows from the observation that
\mathscr{O}^{t+1}=\gamma^{t}
.

For
j=0
the claim holds trivially since
\gamma^{0}=\mathscr{O}
.
Assume now that the claim holds for
j-1
, i.e., that
\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{j-1}\}\cup\{\gamma^{j-1}\}
is equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}\}
.
Since it holds that
\mathscr{O}^{j}=\pi_{1}(\gamma^{j-1},i,z_{j},X)
and
\gamma^{j}=\pi_{2}(\gamma^{j-1},i,z_{j},X)
,
it holds again by
Corollary

105
that
\{\mathscr{O}^{1},\dots,\mathscr{O}^{j}\}\cup\{\gamma^{j}\}
is equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}\}
.
∎

Now we define the procedure
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
that combines the two operations above.

Procedure 7
.

The operation
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
takes as input a tuple
(\mathscr{O},i)
, where
\mathscr{O}\coloneq(\iso,O,b)
is an ordered pattern and
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}}
. Let
\{(\mathscr{O}^{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{t+1},p_{t+1})\}=\operatorname{ShiftVectorL}(\mathscr{O},i)
, where
(\mathscr{O}^{t+1},p_{t+1})
is the unique pair such that
p_{t+1}=\perp
.
Let
\{(\tilde{\mathscr{O}}^{1},\tilde{p}_{1}),\dots,(\tilde{\mathscr{O}}^{s+1},\tilde{p}_{s+1})\}=\operatorname{ShiftVectorI}(\mathscr{O}^{t+1},i)
. Then the operation outputs the set

\{(\mathscr{O}^{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{t},p_{t}),(\tilde{\mathscr{O}}^{1},\tilde{p}_{1}),\dots,(\tilde{\mathscr{O}}^{s+1},\tilde{p}_{s+1})\}.

Then the following statement is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

108
.

Corollary 109
.

Given an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}
and an index
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}}
, let
P
be the set resulting from
\operatorname{ShiftVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
and let
S=\{\mathscr{O}\colon(\mathscr{O},p)\in P\}
. Then
S
is equivalent to
\mathscr{O}
.

The following observation follows by Observations
107
and
106
, since the union operation preserves equivalence.

Observation 110
.

Let
(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},p)
be some pair in the output of
\operatorname{ShiftVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. Then all following hold:

•

either
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
or
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=(I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\})\cup p
for each value
X\in\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}
; and

•

b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
.

In particular, it holds for the unique pair
(\mathscr{O},p)
with
p=\perp
that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
.

Now we define the recursive operation
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
that exhaustively applies the operation
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
to remove a single bad index, without creating further bad indices.

Procedure 8
.

The recursive operation
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
takes as input an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}
over
m
pairs, an index
i\in[m]
, and outputs a family of ordered patterns
C
, defined as follows.
If
i\notin I_{\mathscr{O}}
, we define
C\coloneq\{\mathscr{O}\}
.
Otherwise, let
\{(\mathscr{O}^{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{t+1},p_{t+1})\}=\operatorname{ShiftVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
, and let
(\mathscr{O}^{t+1},p_{t+1})
be the unique pair with
p_{t+1}=\perp
.
Then for
j\in[t]
we define the sets
C_{j}\coloneq\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{j},p_{j})
.
Finally, we define
C\coloneq\{\mathscr{O}^{t+1}\}\cup\bigcup_{j\in[t]}C_{j}
.

Now we prove some properties of this operation that we need later to show the correctness of our procedures. We start with the following definition:

Definition 111
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an ordered pattern over
m
pairs and
i\in[m]
.
We define the tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
as the recursion tree of the operation
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
(we omit the subscript
\mathscr{O}
when clear from context).
We also label each node of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
corresponding to a call
(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},i^{\prime})
with the index
i^{\prime}
.

Observation 112
.

Let
\mathscr{O}=(\iso,O,b)
and
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
be ordered patterns such that
(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},i^{\prime})
is a child of the root of the recursion tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
.
It holds that:

1.

(O^{\prime})_{i}
is a unit pair. In particular, it holds that
i\notin I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}
;

2.

(b^{\prime})^{-1}(i)=\emptyset
;

3.

i^{\prime}=b(q,X)
for some bad coordinate
(q,X)
of
i
with respect to
b
; and

4.

b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
.

Lemma 113
.

There cannot exist two distinct nodes on the same root-leaf path of the recursion tree
\mathcal{T}_{i}
sharing the same label
j\in[m]
.

Proof.

It holds by induction over the depth of the nodes in the recursion tree, using item (2) of
Observation

112
, that for a node corresponding to a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},i^{\prime})
, and for a descendant node corresponding to a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime},i^{\prime\prime})
, it holds that
(b^{\prime\prime})^{-1}(i^{\prime})=\emptyset
. The lemma follows then by item (3) of
Observation

112
.
∎

Lemma 114
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
be the recursion tree of a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
.
Then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
has at most
(2k)^{k}
nodes. In particular, the procedure runs in time
k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, and it holds that
|\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O},i)|\leq k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Proof.

A call of
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
makes at most
|Z_{\mathscr{O}}(i)|\leq 2k
recursive calls, and hence, each node of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
has at most
2k
children. Moreover, by
Lemma

113
, the labels assigned to the nodes of each root-leaf path of the recursion tree are pairwise distinct. Hence,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
has height at most
k
. It follows that
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
has at most
(2k)^{k}
nodes. The bound on the running time follows then, since each node of the recursion tree, aside from the recursive calls, only makes a call of
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
, which runs in polynomial time.

Now let
T_{h}
be the maximum size of a family returned by a call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
with a recursion tree of height
h
. Then it holds that
T_{0}=1
, and for
h\geq 1
, it holds that
T_{h}\leq k\cdot T_{h-1}+1\leq(k+1)T_{h-1}
. This implies that
T_{k}\leq(k+1)^{k}\leq k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
∎

Lemma 115
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an ordered pattern over
m
pairs,
i\in[m]
. Then the operation
\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O},i)
produces a family of ordered patterns equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}\}
, such that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
and
b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
for each pattern
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
in the resulting family.

Proof.

Let
(\mathscr{O}^{0},i_{0}),\dots,(\mathscr{O}^{r},i_{r})
with be some sequence of calls of the
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
operation corresponding to some root-leaf path of the recursion tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathscr{O},i}
.
For
t\in[r]_{0}
, let
\mathscr{O}^{t}=(\iso,O^{t},b^{t})
.
Note that
i_{0},\dots,i_{r}
are the labels assigned to the nodes of this path. Then in each iteration, the coordinates of
i_{j}
are shifted to either their big indices or to
i_{j+1}
.
Hence,
i_{j+1}
is the only index that can turn bad in
O^{j+1}
with respect to
b^{j+1}
, i.e.,
I_{\mathscr{O}^{j+1}}\subseteq(I_{\mathscr{O}^{j}}\setminus\{i_{j}\})\cup\{i_{j+1}\}
.
It follows by induction, that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{r}}\subseteq I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus\{i\}
holds for the last pattern
(\mathscr{O}^{r})
in the sequence.

Moreover, it holds by
Observation

112
,
b^{j+1}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b^{j}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
, and hence,
b^{r}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
holds also by induction.
The equivalence of the families also holds by induction using
Corollary

109
, since we only replace a pattern with the family resulting from an application of
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
on this pattern.
∎

Finally, we define the
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
operation that, given a pattern
A
, outputs a family of nice patterns equivalent to
A
. The first step of this operation is a subroutine called
\operatorname{ProcessBig}
that takes care of the big vectors, i.e., ensures that the big vectors have no bad coordinates.

Procedure 9
.

We define the subroutine
\operatorname{ProcessBig}
that takes an ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}
as input, and outputs a family of ordered patterns as follows:
Let
H_{0}=\{\mathscr{O}\}
. We compute
H_{j}
from
H_{j-1}
for
j\in[k]
as follows:
For each pattern
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in H_{j-1}
, we define the set
C_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}
, where
C_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\coloneq\{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\}
if
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})=\emptyset
, and
C_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\coloneq\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},i)
for the smallest
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
otherwise.

We define
H_{j}=\bigcup_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in H_{j-1}}C_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}
. Let
s
be the smallest integer such that
H_{j}=H_{j+1}
. The operation outputs the family
H_{s}
.

Lemma 116
.

It holds for each ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}=(\iso,O,b)
, that
\operatorname{ProcessBig}(\mathscr{O})
runs in time
k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
and outputs a family
H
of ordered patterns equivalent to
\mathscr{O}
, such that for each pattern
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in H
, it holds that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})=\emptyset
,
b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
and
O_{j}=O^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\notin b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
. Moreover, the process finishes after at most
2k
iterations.

Proof.

We start with the last claim.
For each ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in H_{j}
with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime},i)
for some pattern pattern
\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}\in H_{j-1}
, it holds that
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k],X)
. By
Lemma

115
,
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}}\setminus\{i\}
. Hence,
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k],\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
is strictly smaller than
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}}
. Therefore, it holds by item (3) of
Observation

112
that the largest size of such a set over all patterns in
H_{j}
decreases after each iteration. Hence, it must hold after
2k
iterations for all patterns
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in H_{j}
that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\cap b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})=\emptyset
.

The claim
b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
holds by
Observation

112
. The claim
O_{j}=O_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\notin b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
also holds by
Observation

112
,
since we only call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on indices in
b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
, which only shifts indices to
b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
.
H_{j}
is equivalent to
H_{j-1}
by
Lemma

115
, since we only replace a pattern with the family resulting from an application of
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on this pattern. It follows by induction over
j
that
H
is equivalent to
\mathscr{O}
.

We claim that
|H_{j}|\leq k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
for all
j\geq 0
. The lemma then follows by
Lemma

114
. For
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in H_{j}
with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
, by
Lemma

115
for each index
i\in I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}
,
O^{\prime}_{i}=O_{i}
. This holds since each time we shift to an index
i
(possibly making it a bad index), we directly call
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on
i
, and hence, by
Lemma

115
,
i
cannot be a bad index in the patterns resulting from such a call. Therefore, each pattern of
H_{j}
can be uniquely identified by the indices of
b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
that were “reduced”, and by their respective values. Let
R=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}
be the set of these indices that do not correspond to unit or zero pairs.
Let
(u_{i_{1}},S_{i_{1}}),(u_{i_{2}},S_{i_{2}}),\dots,(u_{i_{r}},S_{i_{r}})
be the pairs corresponding to the indices in
R
.
Since all the indices of
R
are not bad indices, the non-zero coordinates of the
u_{i_{j}}
’s for
j\in[r]
must form a partition of a subset of
[k]
; likewise, every element of
[k]
appears in at most one set among
S_{i_{1}},\dots,S_{i_{r}}
.
Hence, we have at most
3^{(2k)}\cdot(2k)^{(2k)}\cdot(2k)^{(2k)}\cdot(2k+1)^{(2k)}=k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
possible patterns in
H_{j}
, where the first term counts for each index
i\in b([k]\times\{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{L}\})
whether
O^{\prime}_{i}=O_{i}
,
O^{\prime}_{i}
is a unit or zero pair, or
i\notin I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}
and
O_{i}
is not a unit or zero vector.
The second term counts the number of partitions of the non-zero coordinates, while the third term counts the number of ways to distribute
[k]
among
S_{i_{1}},\dots,S_{i_{r}}
.
Finally, the last term counts the different unit and zero pairs for each index corresponding to a unit or zero vector
O^{\prime}_{i}
.
∎

Now we are ready to describe the procedure
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
, that takes as input a pattern and outputs a family of nice patterns equivalent to the input.

Procedure 10
.

The procedure
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
takes as input a pattern
A\in\mathcal{P}
, and outputs a family of patterns.
The procedure first fixes an arbitrary ordered pattern
\mathscr{O}_{A}\coloneqq(\iso,O,b)
of
A
. It computes the family
S_{0}=\operatorname{ProcessBig}(\mathscr{O}_{A})
.

Let
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\}\coloneqq I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
.
For each value
j\in[s]
, we define
S_{j}
as follows: First, let
S^{\prime}_{j}=\bigcup_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in S_{j-1}}\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{\prime},i_{j})
.
Then the family
S_{j}
results from
S^{\prime}_{j}
as follows: we iterate over all patterns
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{j}
, and we add
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
to
S_{j}
if the following two conditions hold: first, if
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
does not contain any zero pair, and second if
S_{j}
does not already contain a pattern
\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}
, that shares the same underlying pattern with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
, i.e.,
p(\mathscr{O}^{\prime})=p(\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime})
. In other words, for each pattern
p\in\mathcal{P}
, we only add one “representative” ordered pattern of
p
to
S_{j}
if such a pattern exists in
S^{\prime}_{j}
.
The operation outputs the family

\big\{p(\mathscr{O})\colon\mathscr{O}\in S_{s}\big\}
.

\blacktriangle

In the following we show that this subroutine runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, and correctly produces a family of nice patterns equivalent to the input pattern.

Lemma 117
.

It holds for all
j\in[s]_{0}
and
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{j}
with
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
and
b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
. Further, it holds that
O^{\prime}_{\ell}=O_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction over
j\in[s]
.
For
j=0
, the claim holds by
Lemma

116
, since
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=I_{\mathscr{O}}\setminus b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
for all
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in S_{0}
. Moreover,
b^{\prime}([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})\subseteq b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
holds since each pattern resulting from
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
only shifts big vector assignments to big indices in
b([k]\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\})
.

Now assume the claim holds for
j-1
. Let
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}=(\iso,O^{\prime},b^{\prime})
be a pattern in
S^{\prime}_{j}
, and let
\mathscr{O}^{*}=(\iso,O^{*},b^{*})
be a pattern in
S_{j-1}
such that
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ReduceVector}(\mathscr{O}^{*},i^{\mathbf{L}}_{j})
.
Then by
Lemma

115
, we have that

I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{\mathscr{O}^{*}}\setminus\{i_{j}\}
and
b^{\prime}([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])\subseteq b^{*}([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])
. Moreover, it holds by the induction hypothesis that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{*}}=\{i_{j},\dots,i_{s}\}
, and
b^{*}([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])\subseteq b([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])
. It follows that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}\subseteq\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
, and
b^{\prime}([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])\subseteq b([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])
.

Finally, the inclusion
\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}\subseteq I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}
follows from the fact that
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
only shifts to big indices, and that it holds by induction hypothesis that
b^{*}([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])\subseteq b([k\times\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{I}\}])
is distinct from all indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{s}
. By the same observation, we also have that
O^{\prime}_{i_{\ell}}=O^{*}_{i_{\ell}}=O_{i_{\ell}}
for all
\ell\in\{j+1,\dots,s\}
.
∎

Corollary 118
.

For each
A\in\mathcal{P}
, the operation
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
produces a family of nice patterns equivalent to
A
.

Proof.

It holds by
Lemma

117
that
I_{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for all
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\in\bar{S}_{\bar{s}}
. Since we remove all patterns containing a zero pair, by
Observation

100
, the output family is nice.

It holds by
Lemma

116
that
S_{0}
is equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}_{A}\}

We show that
S_{j}
is equivalent to
S_{j-1}
for all
j\in[s]
, and hence, it hold by induction over
j\in[s]_{0}
that
S_{j}
is equivalent to
\{\mathscr{O}_{A}\}
.
It holds that
S^{\prime}_{j}
is equivalent to
S_{j-1}
for all
j\in[s]
by
Lemma

115
. The family
S_{j}
is then equivalent to
S^{\prime}_{j}
by the fact that we only remove patterns that are either equivalent to other patterns in the family, or contain at least a zero pair. Since the latter patterns are not compatible with any partial solution, they are equivalent to the empty family, and they can be removed safely.
∎

Lemma 119
.

For each
j\in[s]_{0}
,
|S_{j}|\leq|\mathcal{P^{*}}|\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
Similarly, for each
j\in[\bar{s}]_{0}
,
|\bar{S}_{j}|\leq|\mathcal{P^{*}}|\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Proof.

For
j=0
this holds by
Lemma

116
. Now assume
j>0
.
By
Lemma

117
, all patterns in
S_{j}
have the same set of bad indices and identical vectors at bad indices, i.e., it holds for all
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in S_{j}
,
I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}
, and for
i\in I_{O^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
,
O^{\prime}_{i}=O_{i}
.
Let
S_{j}^{*}
be the family resulting from
S_{j}
by removing all pairs
\{O_{i}\colon i\in\{i_{j+1},\dots,i_{s}\}\}
. Then
S_{j}^{*}
contains nice patterns only, each containing at most
n
vectors. Moreover, for any two patterns
(O^{\prime},b^{\prime}),(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\in S_{j}^{*}
, it holds that
p(O^{\prime},b^{\prime})\neq p(O^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, since otherwise, the original ordered patterns in
S_{j}
would share the same underlying pattern, a contradiction to our choice of of
S_{j}
. Hence, the size of
S_{j}^{*}
(and hence, of
S_{j}
) is bounded by the number of nice patterns with at most
n
vectors (i.e.,
|\mathcal{P^{*}}|
), which is at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, by
Observation

97
.
∎

Corollary 120
.

The operation
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
for each pattern
A\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

116
,
\operatorname{ProcessBig}(\mathscr{O})
runs in time
k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
For each
j\in[s]
, we apply
\operatorname{ReduceVector}
on each element of the family
S_{j-1}
. This runs in time
|S_{j-1}|\cdot k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}{\mathsf{poly}}(n)\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
by Lemmas
114
and
119
, since each call of the
\operatorname{ShiftVector}
runs in polynomial time.
Moreover, it holds by these two lemmas that
S^{\prime}_{j}
has size
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. Hence,
S_{j}
can be computed from
S^{\prime}_{j}
in time
(n^{\mathcal{O}(k)})^{2}{\mathsf{poly}}(n)=n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
Since
s\leq n
, the total running time is
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
∎

9.3
The Algorithm

Before defining the algorithm, we describe an auxiliary operator that corresponds to relabeling vertices in the NLC-expression, i.e., it describes how a pattern corresponding to a partial solution changes after a relabel operation.

Definition 121
.

Let
\beta:[k]\to[k]

be some relabeling function. For a vector
v\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we define
\rho_{\beta}(v)=v^{\prime}
, where
v^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j\in\beta^{-1}(i)}v_{j}
, and for a set
S\subseteq[k]
, we define
\rho_{\beta}(S)=\big\{\beta(i)\colon i\in S\big\}
.
Given a pattern
(\iso,\mset)\in\mathcal{P}
, we define

\rho_{\beta}(\iso,\mset)=\big(\rho_{\beta}(\iso),\rho_{\beta}(\mset)\big)
,
where
\rho_{\beta}(\mset)=\big\langle\big(\rho_{\beta}(u),\rho_{\beta}(S)\big)\colon(u,S)\in\mset\big\rangle
.

Now, we describe our algorithm. Intuitively, at each join node
x
of the NLC-decomposition, in order to create all partial solutions at
x
we iterate over all pairs of partial solutions at its children, and for each such pair we iterate over all possible ways of adding edges between them. However, to keep the number of created patterns bounded, we add the edges one by one, applying the
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
procedure on all resulting patterns after each addition. After that we apply the corresponding relabeling procedure on the resulting patterns and make our record nice one final time by applying
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
again.

Along this algorithm, we will deal with
2k
-labeled graphs, distinguishing between the labels of the vertices coming from the first and the second child of a join node. This will allow us to unify the partial solutions from the two children without conflicts, allowing us to add edges between them correctly.

Algorithm 3
.

For each
x\in\mathcal{V}
, we define the families
T_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{*}
recursively over
\mathcal{T}
as follows. For an initial node
\bullet_{i}
, we define
T_{x}=\left\{\big(\mathbf{e}_{i},\langle\rangle\big)\right\}
.

For a join node
x
with children
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, let
\alpha\subseteq[k]^{2}
and
\beta:[k]\to[k]
such that
G_{x}=G_{x_{1}}\oplus_{\alpha}^{\beta}G_{x_{2}}
.
The algorithm computes the table
T_{x}
as follows:

First, the algorithm computes the set
T^{\prime}
that corresponds to a representation of all combinations of partial solutions from
T_{x_{1}}
and
T_{x_{2}}
after applying the join operation
\oplus_{\alpha}
. In order to do this, we first define the following sets
T^{(i)}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
\beta_{0}:[2k]\rightarrow[2k]
be the relabeling function defined as
\beta_{0}(i)=i+k
for
i\in[k]
and
\beta_{0}(i)=i
otherwise, and
\beta_{1}:[2k]\rightarrow[2k]
be defined as
\beta_{1}(i)=i
for
i\in[k]
and
\beta_{1}(i)=i-k
otherwise.
Moreover, let
\alpha^{\prime}:=\{(x,\beta_{0}(y))\colon(x,y)\in\alpha\}
.
We define the set

T^{(0)}:=\bigcup_{(A_{1},A_{2})\in T_{x_{1}}\times T_{x_{2}}}\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(\repr(F_{A_{1}}\dot{\cup}\rho_{\beta_{0}}(F_{A_{2}}))).

This forms all disjoint unions of partial solutions from the two children, where the labels of the second child are shifted by
k
.
For
z>0
, we define the sets
T^{(z)}
from
T^{(z-1)}
as follows:
First, we define
T^{(z)}_{0}
, where for each pattern
A\in T^{(z-1)}
, and for each pair
\{u,v\}\in\big(V(F_{A})\times V(F_{A})\big)\setminus E(F_{A})

with
(\lab_{F_{A}}(u),\lab_{F_{A}}(v))\in\alpha^{\prime}

and such that the graph
F^{\prime}
resulting from adding the edge
\{u,v\}
to
F_{A}
is acyclic, the algorithm adds
\repr(F^{\prime})
to
T_{0}^{(z)}
. Finally, the algorithm defines
T^{(z)}=\bigcup_{A\in T_{0}^{(z)}}\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
.

We define the sets
T^{(z)}_{R}
for each value
z\geq 0
as the set
T^{(z)}_{R}=\rho_{\beta_{1}}(T^{(z)})
, and
T^{(z)}_{N}=\bigcup_{A\in T^{(z)}_{R}}\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
.
Let
r
be the smallest integer such that
T^{(r)}=T^{(r+1)}
. The algorithm computes
T^{\prime}=\bigcup_{z\in[r]_{0}}T^{(z)}_{N}
.
Finally, the algorithm computes the set
T^{\prime\prime}=\bigcup_{A\in T^{\prime}}\rho_{\beta}(A)
and the set
T_{x}=\bigcup_{A\in T^{\prime\prime}}\operatorname{ReduceToNice}(A)
.
The algorithm outputs YES if
T_{r}
contains a solution pattern
\elpat
at the root node
r
, or outputs NO otherwise.

\blacktriangle

Now we show that the computed set
T_{x}
is equivalent to the record
\mathcal{R}_{x}
at each node
x\in\mathcal{V}
. First, we prove that adding a single edge between two labels preserves the representation of partial solutions. This lemma requires the finer notion of compatibility defined in
Definition

91
, which is essential for proving the correctness of the algorithm. We also show that the relabeling operation preserves equivalence.

Lemma 122
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
be two families of patterns such that
\mathcal{R}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{2}
. For
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
, we define the families
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}
, where for each pattern
A\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
and for each pair
(u,v)
such that
\lab_{F_{A}}(u)=i
,
\lab_{F_{A}}(v)=j
,
uv\notin E(F_{A})
, and the graph
F^{\prime}
obtained from
F_{A}
by adding the edge
uv
, we add
\repr(F^{\prime})
to
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
. We define
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}
similarly from
\mathcal{R}_{2}
.
Then, it holds that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
F^{*}
be a fixed forest such that there exists
F^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}
that is
(\delta^{\prime},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
. We need to show that there exists
F^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
that is
(\delta^{\prime},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
. The other direction then follows by symmetry.

Let
F^{**}
be the forest obtained from
F^{*}
by adding two new vertices
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
with labels
k+1
and
k+2
with an edge between them, and let
\delta:[k+2]\times[k+2]\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
be the extension of
\delta^{\prime}
by setting
\delta(i,k+1)=\delta(j,k+2)=1
and
\delta(t,t^{\prime})=0
for all other values of
t
and
t^{\prime}
with
\{t,t^{\prime}\}\cap\{k+1,k+2\}\neq\emptyset
.

We claim that a fixed forest
F
is
(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with the forest
F^{**}
if and only if there exists a forest
F^{\prime}
that is
(\delta^{\prime},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
, such that
F^{\prime}
is obtained by adding an edge between a vertex labeled
i
and a vertex labeled
j
in
F
.

Before we prove the claim, we show that the lemma follows from this claim. Since
F^{\prime}_{2}
is
(\delta^{\prime},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
, there exists by this claim a forest
F_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{2}
that is
(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with
F^{**}
. But since
\mathcal{R}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{2}
, there exists a forest
F_{1}
in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
that is
(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with
F^{**}
. It follows again by the claim above that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
contains a forest
F_{1}^{\prime}
that is
(\delta^{\prime},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
which proves the lemma.

Now we prove the claim. Let
T
be a
(\delta,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F
and
F^{**}
. In
T
,
w_{1}
has exactly one edge to a vertex in
F
of label
i
, and similarly
w_{2}
has exactly one edge to a vertex in
F
of label
j
. Let
u
and
v
be these two vertices respectively.
By replacing the path
uw_{1}w_{2}v
path with the edge
\{u,v\}
, we obtain a
(\delta^{\prime},\ell)
-spanning tree
T^{\prime}
for the forest
F^{\prime}
obtained from
F
. The other direction of the claim follows by reversing this argument, where we replace the edge
\{u,v\}
added to create
F^{\prime}
in
T^{\prime}
with the path
uw_{1}w_{2}v
. Note that the number of leaves is the same in
T
and
T^{\prime}
, since the degrees of
u
and
v
do not change, and since
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
both have degree two in
T
, and hence, are not leaves.
∎

Lemma 123
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
be two families of patterns such that
\mathcal{R}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. For a relabeling function
\beta:[k]\to[k]
, we define the families
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}:=\rho_{\beta}(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}:=\rho_{\beta}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
.
Then, it holds that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta\in\mathcal{A}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
F^{*}
be a fixed forest such that there exists
A^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
with
F_{A^{\prime}_{1}}

(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
. We need to show that there exists
A^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}
such that
F_{A^{\prime}_{2}}
is
(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
. The other direction then follows by symmetry.

For a fixed forest
F
and for
F^{\prime}=\rho_{\beta}(F)
, we claim that
F^{\prime}
is
(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
if and only if there exists a mapping
\delta_{0}
such that
\delta(i,j)=\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k]\\
\beta(i^{\prime})=i}\delta_{0}(i^{\prime},j)
for all
i,j\in[k]
, and
F
is
(\delta_{0},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
. Before we prove the claim, we show that the lemma follows from this claim. Since
F_{A^{\prime}_{1}}
is
(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
, there exists by this claim a mapping
\delta_{0}
and a forest
F_{A_{1}}
in
\mathcal{R}
that is
(\delta_{0},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
. But since
\mathcal{R}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, there exists a forest
F_{A_{2}}
in
\mathcal{R}_{2}
that is
(\delta_{0},\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
. It follows again by the claim above that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}
contains a pattern
A_{2}
where
F_{A^{\prime}_{2}}
is
(\delta,\ell)
-compatible with
F^{*}
which proves the lemma.

Now we prove the claim. Let
T
be a
(\delta,\ell)
-spanning tree for
F^{\prime}
and
F^{*}
. Then the tree
T_{0}
resulting from
T
by reverting the labels of the vertices of
F^{\prime}
to their original label in
F
is a
(\delta_{0},\ell)
-spanning tree for
F
and
F^{*}
, where
\delta_{0}
is defined as the number of cut edges between each pair of labels between
F
and
F^{*}
in
T_{0}
. It is not hard to see that
\delta
and
\delta_{0}
satisfy the relation in the claim. The other direction of the claim follows by reversing this argument, where we relabel the vertices of
F
in
T_{0}
according to
\beta
to obtain
F^{\prime}
, and we define
\delta
as in the claim.
∎

Lemma 124
.

It holds for each
x\in\mathcal{V}
that
T_{x}
is equivalent to the record
\mathcal{R}_{x}
, i.e.,
T_{x}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x}
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by induction over
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
x
be a
\bullet_{i}
node and let
v
be the only vertex of
G_{x}
.
Clearly,
G_{x}
admits a single partial solution, namely, the graph
G_{x}
itself, consisting of the isolated vertex
v
. Hence, it holds by the definition of
\repr
that
\mathcal{R}_{x}=T_{x}
.

For a join node
x\in\mathcal{V}
, let
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
be the children of
x
.
Let
\alpha_{0}=\{(i,j+k)\colon(i,j)\in\alpha\}
, and
\hat{G}=G_{x_{1}}\oplus_{\alpha_{0}}\rho_{\beta_{0}}(G_{x_{2}})
. Let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
be the record of
\hat{G}
. By the induction hypothesis,
T_{x_{1}}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x_{1}}
and
T_{x_{2}}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x_{2}}
.

Claim 125
.

It holds that
T^{\prime}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

{claimproof}

[Proof of
Claim

125
]
Let
\mathcal{R}^{(0)}
be the record corresponding to the disjoint union of all pairs of partial solutions from
\mathcal{R}_{x_{1}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{x_{2}}
.
We define the families
\mathcal{R}^{(z)}
defined as the sets of all partial solutions that result from adding exactly
z
edges between vertices whose labels belong to
\alpha_{0}
to the partial solutions in
\mathcal{R}^{(0)}
.

We claim that
T^{(z)}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{(z)}
holds for all values of
z
. Before we prove the claim, we note that
Claim

125
follows from it by
Corollary

118
, since it follows from the definition of equivalence and by
Lemma

123
that both the union operation and relabeling preserve equivalence.

Now we prove the claim by induction over
z
.
It holds by
Corollary

118
that
T^{(0)}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{(0)}
. Now assume that
T^{(z-1)}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{(z-1)}
holds for some
z>0
. It holds by
Lemma

122
that the partial solution resulting from adding a single edge between two labels preserves equivalence. Again, since it holds that the union operation preserves equivalence, and
\mathcal{R}^{(z)}
can be defined from
\mathcal{R}^{(z-1)}
by the union over all
(i,k+j)\in\alpha_{0}
of adding a single edge between a vertex of label
i
and a vertex of label
k+j
to some partial solution, and since
T_{0}^{(z)}
results from
T^{(z-1)}
in the same way, it holds that
T^{(z)}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{(z)}
.

Hence, it follows from the transitivity of equivalence, and from
Corollary

118
that
T^{(z)}\simeq T^{(z)}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{R}^{(z)}
, which completes the proof.

Since
\mathcal{R}_{x}
results from
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
by applying the relabeling function
\beta
, it holds by
Lemma

123
that
T^{\prime\prime}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x}
.
Again, it follows from
Corollary

118
that
T_{x}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{x}
.
∎

We now characterize the YES-instances:

Corollary 126
.

The instance
(G,\eltarget)
is a YES-instance if and only if
T_{r}
contains a solution pattern.

Proof.

Let
\elpat^{\prime}
be the empty pattern. Then it holds that
\elpat
is
(\emptyset,\eltarget)
-compatible with
\elpat^{\prime}
, if and only if
\elpat
is a solution pattern.
Since it holds by
Lemma

124
that
T_{r}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{R}_{r}
, it holds that
T_{r}
contains a solution pattern if and only if
\mathcal{R}_{r}
contains such a pattern. Finally, it holds by
Observation

90
that this is the case if and only if
G
is a YES-instance.
∎

Finally, we bound the running time of our algorithm:

Lemma 127
.

Algorithm
3
runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Proof.

Since the size of the decomposition tree is polynomial in
n
, it suffices to bound the running time for a single node
x
. This is trivial for a leaf node. For an inner node
x
with children
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, since
T_{x_{1}}
and
T_{x_{2}}
contain at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
patterns each, by
Corollary

120
, the computation of
T^{(0)}
takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. Since it holds by
Corollary

118
that
T^{(z)}
contains only nice patterns, and since for each such pattern we iterate over at most
n^{2}
different ways of adding a single edge, and since we call
\operatorname{ReduceToNice}
once after adding each edge, it follows by
Corollary

120
that the computation of each
T^{(z)}
takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
for each value of
z
. Since
r
is bounded by the number of added edges, and hence, by
n^{2}
, computing
T^{\prime}
can be done in
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. Since relabeling a single pattern can be done in polynomial time, also
T^{\prime\prime}
can be computed in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. Thus, by
Corollary

120
, computing
T_{x}
takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the main theorem of this section:

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Given an instance
(G,\eltarget)
of
Exact Leaf Spanning Tree
together with an NLC-decomposition of width
k
, we run Algorithm
3
. By Corollary
126
, the input instance is a YES-instance if and only if
T_{r}
contains a solution pattern. The total running time is
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, by Lemma
127
.
∎

10
Concluding Remarks

Our results not only provide a comprehensive and in-depth overview of the fine-grained complexity of computing a fundamental class of spanning trees, but also develop new techniques which we believe are applicable to other problems, including those related to graph factoring.
One task left for future work is closing the small remaining gap between the upper bound for treewidth (Theorem
5
) and the lower bound for pathwidth (Theorem
6
); presently, it seems difficult to obtain further progress in either of these two directions. We remark that a similar gap also exists for the classical
Hamiltonian Cycle
problem
[
15
]
, which has a tight
\mathcal{O}^{*}((2+\sqrt{2})^{\pw(G)})
lower and upper bound, but no matching tight bound for treewidth is known.

Alternatively, one may ask whether using more restrictive structural parameterizations—such as the
treedepth
of the input graph—can be used to design fixed-parameter algorithms for instances with large
r
, similarly as was achieved in Theorem
4
.
Finally, a long-standing open question in the field
[
17
,
47
]
is whether Cut&Count based algorithms can be derandomized while achieving the same asymptotic running time. To the best of our knowledge, all known deterministic techniques for connectivity problems parameterized by pathwidth (and treewidth) yield single exponential running time but with a blow up in the base of the exponent
[
2
,
54
,
24
]
.
